GTA 6's map is so big it's going to take months for people to comb through it and find everything. Rockstar has already posted the full map of Leonida online, but it doesn't answer one simple question that I know we've all been wondering: How long would it take to fly across this entire thing in a plane?

The answer is a lot less time than it would take to go by car, but still long enough to give you an idea of the sheer scale we're working with here. Using the closest plane to Jason's house, my trip across Leonida took around 4 minutes and 18 seconds, which is a little under an hour of in-game time. You can watch the entire flight from start to finish below.

Keep in mind that the Mammoth Dodo seaplane that carried me from the southern tip of the archipelago to the northern tip of the map is just one type of plane in GTA 6. I would bet there are faster options if you really want to zoom.

Even though I'm still very early in the GTA 6's story, I didn't have to go too far out of my way to test this. Jason's house is conveniently located pretty close to a spot with perfectly unattended planes to borrow. The Sunbelt Highway that runs south of it will lead you right to the earliest planes and helicopters you can find. Just don't fly over the nearby military base (trust me, it's bad news) and you'll be fine.

Rockstar compared GTA 6's map to its previous games, describing it as about the size of GTA 4, GTA 5, and Red Dead Redemption 2 combined. Despite knowing this beforehand, I was still shocked to see how the map kept going when I first opened it up. Four minutes might not sound like a lot, but the enormity of it all sinks in as you glide above densely populated cities and neighborhoods that could be the setting of their own standalone videogames.

My experiment has left the fog of war on my map split into two halves. My flight path carved a line of partially revealed locations straight through it. I don't expect to clear the rest out for dozens of hours, but now I'm tempted to find out how long of a roadtrip it would be to do the same journey by car.

This Is What Was on the Menu at the First Thanksgiving in 1621

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Somehow, turkey has spent the last four centuries getting all the glory.

For a meal that has been discussed, depicted and reenacted for more than four centuries, the first Thanksgiving has left us surprisingly few details about what anyone actually ate. We know there were roughly 140 people, three days of feasting and exactly five deer. Beyond that, the historical record is rather unhelpful. Apparently, nobody thought to write down the menu.

The gathering took place in Plymouth in the fall of 1621, when about 50 English colonists celebrated their first successful harvest alongside 90 Wampanoag, including their leader, Ousamequin, better known to history as Massasoit. It was a meeting between two communities whose relationship was far more complicated than the cheerful scene that would eventually find its way into American classrooms. Although they had formed an alliance earlier that year, cultural differences and tensions of an English settlement on Wampanoag land were hardly going to disappear over dinner.

And while the English had much to celebrate in bringing in their first harvest, they owed a great deal of that success to Indigenous knowledge. The Wampanoag understood the region’s growing seasons, its waterways and the foods that could be grown and gathered in the surrounding landscape. Their traditions had helped sustain communities there long before the colonists arrived.

The two surviving English accounts of the gathering offer only a few details about the feast itself, but taken together with what historians know about the food traditions of both communities, they give us a rather interesting picture of what may have been on the table.

Edward Winslow, one of the English colonists who attended the gathering, recorded that the Wampanoag went hunting and returned with five deer, which they presented to the colony’s leaders. It’s one of the few specific foods mentioned in his account and an especially substantial contribution to a celebration that lasted three days.

Deer were an important source of food for the Wampanoag, who also made use of their hides, bones and other materials. In England, however, deer hunting was largely reserved for the wealthy, making venison something most ordinary people rarely got to enjoy. The meat may have been roasted over a fire or simmered in a hearty venison stew, although Winslow neglected to tell us how it was prepared. We know exactly how many deer arrived, but what became of them in the kitchen remains a mystery.

Roast turkey has become so thoroughly associated with Thanksgiving that it’s difficult to imagine the holiday without one occupying the center of the table. The trouble is, we don’t actually know whether turkey was served at the original celebration.

Winslow wrote that four Englishmen went hunting for wildfowl and returned with enough to feed the colonists for nearly a week. What he didn’t do was tell us which birds they shot. Wild turkeys were present in the region, but so were ducks, geese and other game birds, all perfectly suitable for roasting over a fire. There’s every possibility that turkey was on the menu, but nothing to suggest it enjoyed the distinction we’ve since bestowed upon it. For all we know, the centerpiece was a duck, and we’ve spent the last few centuries giving credit to the wrong bird.

Corn was one of the most important foods available to both communities, although the English were relatively new to growing it. The Wampanoag had cultivated maize for generations, developing agricultural practices suited to the region’s soil and growing seasons. Their knowledge was enormously valuable to the colonists, whose first attempts at establishing a food supply had been anything but fruitful.

One traditional Wampanoag preparation was nasaump, a thick porridge made from ground corn, sometimes cooked with other ingredients. Corn could also be ground into meal for simple breads, early relatives of the cornbread we bake today, or used to thicken stews and make other hearty dishes.

The waters around Plymouth were full of food, provided you knew where to look and how to catch it. The Wampanoag had long fished the region’s rivers and coastline, gathering shellfish and catching everything from bass and cod to eels. Clams, mussels and lobsters were plentiful too.

The English were beginning to take advantage of these resources by 1621. William Bradford, the Plymouth colony’s governor, wrote of the colonists catching substantial quantities of cod and bass during the summer. A freshly caught fish could be roasted or boiled, while clams and mussels might be cooked over a fire. Lobster was another plentiful local catch that was enjoyed regularly. The variety of ocean fare available might have made for a Thanksgiving spread that looked rather more like a seafood boil than the feast we know today.

Squash and pumpkin were important crops in Wampanoag agriculture, and their ability to keep well after harvest made them especially useful as the colder weather set in. Both could be roasted, boiled or added to stews, much like we still prepare winter squash today, though without the benefit of a balsamic glaze.

The English called pumpkins pompions, a name I think we abandoned prematurely. They were familiar with the vegetable, although the varieties growing in New England differed from those they might have seen at home. A pumpkin dish would have been entirely plausible at the harvest celebration, but anyone hoping for a slice of pumpkin pie would have to wait almost two centuries. The colonists had limited supplies of wheat flour, butter and sugar, and the familiar custard-filled pie wouldn’t appear in American cookbooks until 1796.

The Wampanoag grew beans alongside corn and squash, three crops that complemented each other remarkably well. Cornstalks supported climbing beans, while squash vines spread across the ground and helped shade the soil. The combination was as useful in the garden as it was on the plate.

A traditional Wampanoag stew called sobaheg combines ingredients like corn, beans, squash and meat or fish, depending on what’s available or in season. The combination of corn and beans also appears in succotash, another dish with Indigenous New England roots that remains an American favorite.

The forests and wetlands of New England offered plenty beyond cultivated crops. Chestnuts, walnuts, hazelnuts, acorns, wild grapes and cranberries were among the foods the Wampanoag knew how to gather and prepare, each with its own season and uses.

Nuts could be eaten plain, stored or ground into meal, while fruits could be enjoyed fresh or preserved for later. Acorns required particular attention, since their natural bitterness had to be leached out with water before they could be used in cooking. It’s one of the few ingredients we’ve more or less surrendered entirely to the squirrels.

Of all the wild fruits available, cranberries have perhaps the most recognizable place on a modern Thanksgiving table. Their natural tartness eventually found a friend in sugar, giving rise to the cranberry sauce that’s become nearly as much a part of the holiday as the turkey itself.

Kasper Hogh and the missed Celtic Christmas advert trick as Martin O’Neill weighs up penalty dilemma

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Celtic Christmas advert was released on Friday containing the usual good cheer and a little festive pop at Rangers. It did, though, miss an obvious storyboard opportunity.

All I want for Christmas is my first Parkhead goal. A sad-looking, shaggy-haired striker is seen traipsing through the snowy streets of the east end of Glasgow before bumping into a dreadlocked king of kings, who gives him the gift of putting the ball in the net.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Although Henrik Larrson is not involved, Kasper Hogh does in fact feature in the latest two-minute festive promo film, which has been released in conjunction with the Celtic FC Foundation’s Christmas appeal.

Callum McGregor calls out “Oi, youse two!” in the direction of Hogh and Benjamin Nygren. Viljami Sinisalo is more direct: “Kasper!” Nygren turns round, and says: “Who?”

It maybe doesn’t work as well when written down. Hogh – pronounced who - is clearly a good sport. However, as Martin O’Neill has said on a number of occasions, and repeated on Friday at Lennoxtown, at some point strikers need to start scoring goals.

Of course, Hogh thought he had finally bagged his first home goal in the hoops two weekends ago. Nygren set him up with a neat pass into the Dane’s path and seconds later the net billowed. Hogh celebrated with understandable gusto before the dismaying news that it was being ruled out for an innocuous foul in the build-up. “For him, that was a killer,” admitted O’Neill on the eve of the latest home game against St Mirren.

All eyes will be on the team sheet. Will Hogh - yet to score after eight Parkhead appearances - play? Having been dropped from the Danish squad for their most recent games against Portugal and Norway, the striker was given some time off in the recent international window.

“He has been here, we have given him some time….he has had no break at all because where he came from (Norway) they were playing matches throughout (the summer),” explained O’Neill.

The manager made an extraordinary – to use one his favourite words - admission following the recent game against Kilmarnock, when Nygren wasted a spot kick. He claimed he would not have let Hogh take the penalty, which visiting keeper Nico Mantl saved, if the Dane had been on the park at the time.

Despite the ever more urgent need for Hogh to break his duck at home, O’Neill clearly felt the potential mental harm from failing to convert from 12 yards would have been too great a risk. The game, with Celtic winning 1-0 at the time, was also on a knife edge. A pressure penalty might not have been what Hogh needed.

It will be interesting to see what happens against St Mirren if Celtic are awarded another penalty and Hogh is available to take it. One might expect the Dane to demand to be given the ball and put this ridiculous issue to bed. Adding to the intrigue is that Nygren, who has an Achilles knock picked up on international duty with Sweden, might not play. Not that O’Neill trusts him from the spot now in any case.

The manager was not a fan of Nygren’s penalty technique against Kilmarnock. “All I will say is that if Nygren plays, which he may or may not, he won’t take it!” he said. “All this (stuttered) walking up….why not just do an Alan Shearer and blast it into the net!? If you hit the goalkeeper, you’ll take his head off!”

Non-scoring strikers is a theme just now given Oli McBurnie’s continued struggles at international level for Scotland. After just being subbed on, one wondered whether Sebastien Pocognoli might have ordered him to take the penalty against North Macedonia at Hampden Park last week. Again, with the manager’s future prospects possibly resting on the kick, maybe not. John McGinn did the necessary and Pocognoli survives, for the time being.

Hogh won’t want any handouts. He’s a centre forward who thrives on goals. He will feel capable of working it out for himself. No charity, please.

As shown against Kilmarnock, he’s not far away. He missed one very good chance shortly after his chalked-off goal when one-on-one with Mantl. “If he had the first goal he would have taken that on another yard,” insisted O’Neill. “But that looked like a man in a real hurry to try and get his first (Celtic Park) goal.”

Easy going and charming, there are still limits to what Hogh can endure. He wouldn’t be human if it wasn’t having an effect.

“He seems philosophical about it at this minute,” O’Neill reported. “He thinks if he is working really hard for the side then he can get in….he should have taken a tip or two from Duran!”

Camilo Duran is the opposite. With 13 goals in 21 appearances, he has made a flying start to his Celtic career.

Or maybe the aforementioned Larsson can make a visit. Like Hogh, the Swede was not an immediate hit at Parkhead.

O’Neill himself suggested Larsson could have some input - “The best thing for me to do is bring Henrik Larsson over and show him how to hit the ball into the net!” - when the discussion turned to players who initially laboured under great expectations.

Perhaps the late Trevor Francis, his old teammate, was one too? O’Neill was at Nottingham Forest when the striker arrived at the City Ground to become Britain’s first £1 million player. Talk about pressure.

“He took a couple of weeks,” remembered O’Neill. “It didn’t help that he had just been signed for a million pounds and Brian Clough told him to make the tea for everyone, which he did!

“First match, he made the tea for us in the dressing room! That wasn’t exactly what Trevor was expecting.” Hogh, who cost eleven times what Clough paid for Francis, has not been made to suffer such indignities - yet.

What can AI learn from a thousand days of hearing what a baby hears?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Princeton recorded eight babies for their first 1,000 days, and a model trained on one child's audio learned language on the same schedule.

A toddler can pick up hundreds of words just by hearing people talk. Could a computer start from scratch and recognize many of those same words on roughly the same timetable?

Researchers at the Princeton Neuroscience Institute wired up 15 homes with cameras and microphones and recorded babies for most of their waking hours across the first 1,000 days of life.

The researchers turned speech recorded around eight children into clean audio using synthetic voices.

They trained a separate model on each child’s audio in the order the speech was originally recorded.

The models identified recurring sound patterns that corresponded to words.

On average, each model reached the halfway point on a vocabulary checklist within 22 days of the child it was paired with.

“We wanted to turn the usual logic of developmental research around,” said Uri Hasson, a professor of psychology and neuroscience at Princeton who led the study.

“Instead of observing a child for an hour in the laboratory, we asked whether we could build a model capable of learning and immerse it in the child’s actual experience, day after day.”

The families were in New Jersey and spanned a range of incomes and backgrounds. Each home got four to 14 recording devices spread across two to five rooms.

The devices ran 12 to 14 hours a day, from February 2022 to May 2025.

The project gathered roughly 1.2 million hours of audio and video, of which the eight homes analyzed in this paper supplied about 600,000. An automated pipeline worked out where the child was in the house and pulled out the speech happening nearby.

Each child ended up with between 1,900 and 6,600 hours of usable speech, three to seven and a half hours a day. That adds up to 6 to 27 million words per child, over a median of 960 recording days.

Every month from eight months on, caregivers filled out a standard checklist of which words their child understood and said. Those forms are how the researchers knew what each child knew, and when.

Children reached half of the 649-word checklist anywhere from about 12 to 22 months old. That ten-month spread between children is the thing most studies average away.

The model started from random numbers. It had no dictionary, no phonetics, no notion of where one word ends and the next begins, and no text.

The program’s first part chops the incoming sound into 20-millisecond pieces and sorts them into categories it invents for itself. Its second part tries to guess the next piece from what came before.

Whenever a run of pieces becomes reliably predictable, the program files it away as a unit, which is the closest thing it has to a word.

Nothing about that design is specific to English or even to speech. What makes it unusual is the schedule.

The audio arrives in the order it was recorded, one day at a time, rather than shuffled into batches the way an AI language model is normally trained.

At the end of each simulated day, the program gets a sample of speech from earlier days, weighted toward recent ones. The researchers call it replay.

English has 39 phonemes, the sound units that distinguish bat from pat. Children spend their first year tuning in to the ones their own language uses.

Within roughly 12 to 15 months of simulated time, the program had carved out dedicated categories for nearly all of them.

Across the eight children, the models averaged 37.5 phonemes each. The lowest count came from the household with the least recording.

The order it happened in, sounds before words, is the order infants follow.

Words came after. For the child the paper calls Coral, the checklist said she understood 97 words at 12 months, and her model had 91.

Across all eight children, the models and the children hit the same vocabulary milestones within an average of 22 days of each other, with the worst case 56 days.

The models also reproduced the differences between children, landing close to each child’s own halfway age.

They did not match perfectly. The models tended to start learning words a little late and then catch up faster, so the shape of the curve was only partly right.

The surprise in the paper is what happened when the researchers took replay away.

Coral’s household produced something like 10 million words a year, which is a rich environment by any measure. Hearing all of it once, in order, wasn’t enough. With a single pass the program barely learned words at all.

Around 25 to 30 passes over each day’s material was enough, and more than that added very little.

The researchers link this to what sleep is thought to do for memory. A program replaying audio files is not a sleeping infant, and the paper is careful about that.

The amount of recording mattered just as much. Fed only a tenth of Coral’s daily speech, under an hour a day, the program learned none of the checklist words by 24 months.

At 30% of the day, about two and a half hours, it learned 80 of the 649 checklist words.

At 70% it got about nine-tenths of the way to the full result. The rarer words, it seems, only turn up for a listener who is there most of the day.

One limitation shapes everything else. Household recordings are full of clattering dishes, television, and two people talking at once, and the program was not asked to cope with any of that.

Instead the researchers transcribed all the speech around each child, then had a text-to-speech system read the transcripts back as clean audio.

Each household got a mix of eight adult female voices, with 30% of segments read in a voice matched to the child’s mother.

So the word order, the timing and the vocabulary are real, and the sound isn’t. The acoustic problem a baby actually solves, picking one voice out of a noisy room, was left for later work.

Three of the eight children also had shorter recordings, 14 to 20 months of usable input, so their later trajectories are extrapolated rather than modeled.

The program also sees nothing: no faces, no pointing, no object being handed over, which is a large part of how toddlers work out what a word refers to.

The program learned sounds and words. It did not learn grammar, and whether the same machinery would arrive at sentence structure is the open question the authors name.

The team wants to use the setup for questions nobody can put to a real baby. What would less sleep do, or screen speech instead of a parent’s?

Future studies could also add eye contact and the rest of the social world the cameras captured but the model never saw.

Hadas Raviv, a computational cognitive scientist in Princeton’s psychology department who built the analysis pipeline, framed the goal as the opposite of the usual one in artificial intelligence.

“Unlike much of modern AI, which aims for superhuman performance, we’re trying to understand how humans learn and how that learning gives rise to intelligence,” Raviv said.

The full study was published in the journal Science.

Stock Market Today, Nov. 20: Amazon Shares Slide on AI and Mega-Cap Tech Sell-Off

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On Nov. 20, 2026, Amazon shares fell as investors weighed heavy AI spending and margins amid a broad tech selloff.

By Will Healy – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Amazon (AMZN -4.10%), a global e-commerce, cloud, and digital advertising platform, closed at $246.82, down 4.10%. Shares fell on Friday amid an AI and mega-cap tech sell-off, while investors are watching capital spending, margins, and AWS growth.Trading volume reached 72.1M shares, coming in about 79% above its three-month average of 40.2M shares. Amazon.com IPO'd in 1997 and has grown 251,971% since going public.

S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) closed at 6,910, down 3.7%, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -5.28%) finished at 22,617, down 5.28%. Among broadline retail and cloud computing peers, Walmart (WMT +1.51%) closed at $127.95, up 1.52%, while eBay (EBAY -1.39%) closed at $115.59, down 1.39%; Walmart's stronger holiday outlook highlighted steadier consumer demand than Amazon.com's pressure points.

Negative sentiment from both its e-commerce and AWS segments seemed to weigh on Amazon stock today.

Soft consumer spending and an increased need for promotional discounting led it to underperform retail counterparts like Walmart, which, as previously mentioned, rose in today's trading session.

Moreover, capital expenditures (capex) continue to weigh on the stock. The company's plan to spend $220 billion has led to free cash outflows, a concerning sign for a company that generated tens of billions in annual free cash flow until recently. Furthermore, despite a massive amount of liquidity, it has had to turn to the debt markets in a rising-rate environment.

Such concerns were not unique to Amazon, and every stock in the Magnificent Seven, which contend with many of the same challenges, dropped today. Additionally, the Anthropic (ANT -8.50%) IPO has put AI in the spotlight this week. That stock has trended down since opening at $111.50 per share yesterday, likely adding to the negative sentiment surrounding AI.

Admittedly, at a P/E ratio of 20, Amazon stock looks like an increasingly attractive buy, arguably making it the one hyperscaler through 2030 that investors should buy and hold. Nonetheless, with Amazon stock caught in the current downturn, investors should probably move cautiously if they choose to invest.

People Are Complaining About The Secret Lives Of Mormon Wives Spinoff (But I Get It, It's Weird)

Fans Were Speculating About Val’s Relationships On DWTS After Suspension. What An Insider Said

Wait, The Actual Contestants On The Mob Weren't Told The Rules?

Sometimes a text can cause a lot of drama.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, on the 2026 TV schedule, only just debuted as the first spinoff, and immediately had its own drama. Funnily enough, the biggest drama of the season involved O.G. Mormon Wives star Mayci Neeley, who got dragged into the mix when older sister McCall DaPron clashed with some of the new cast members.

Things quickly escalated when McCall made Mayci aware of some drama with co-star Bobbi Althoff, resulting in a text to Jen Affleck saying, “I’m gonna f*** them up,” in regard to the individuals who clashed with her sister. And then, Jen told everyone what Mayci said. After it all became public, it was suddenly so much bigger than just venting between sisters, making someone who had previously been thought of as one of the most grounded and level-headed of the original Mormon Wives series come across like a mean girl.

I’m sure we’ve all said things in a text in the heat of the moment, but Mayci’s words got out for all to see, leading her to realize her mistake in responding in the heat of the moment. She also expressed real regret for having made the statement at all. In a recent apology statement, she said:

To be honest, I’m embarrassed. I’m genuinely embarrassed that I said that. I don’t remember the intention behind it because I know I wasn’t trying to get into a street fight, like a physical fight. It’s genuinely embarrassing. I don’t know why the hell I said that.

Even though the text did make me look at Mayci a bit differently from the impression I’d gotten of her on The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, I also understand wanting to defend a family member and don’t blame her for having a strong reaction to feeling like her sister was being attacked. It all felt a bit middle school, and I’m certainly not making ay excuses for her over-the-top response, but the firsthand experience with some wild drama on her own reality TV series probably led Mayci to have a more heightened reaction than she might otherwise have had.

Anytime you get a group of people together who come from different backgrounds, have them spend long periods of time together, and point cameras at them, you’re bound to get some drama as they get to know each other. It was a bit unusual that this particular drama came from someone not actually in the room where it happened, but it sounds like Mayci has definitely learned a lesson from the fallout. She went on to say:

I am very sorry I said that. I did regret saying that. That is definitely out of character for me. … I’m just going to be accountable for the things that I was upset with myself for. I’ve apologized for it, and moving on.

Largely because of Taylor Frankie Paul, we’ve become accustomed to Mormon Wives being synonymous with drama, especially with the chaos surrounding production of the original series. After the cancellation of her season of The Bachelorette, custody issues with her children, and her announced departure, it immediately turned attention to the new spinoff and the drama in the OC. So, I’m glad that Mayci didn’t seem to waste any time in sharing such an honest reaction to her own behavior.

While we don’t yet know the future of this new series, fellow Mormon Wives star Whitney Leavitt is getting her own reality show. You can check out The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives and its Orange County spinoff with a Hulu subscription while we await news of future seasons, other possible spinoffs, and more headline-making drama.

10 Best Easter Eggs in GTA 6

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Aden Carter is a Contributor at DualShockers who has been covering games professionally since 2020. He specializes in lists and guides, with a focus on RPGs, horror games, survival horror, Soulslikes, cozy/sim games, and action-adventure titles. Before joining DualShockers, Aden contributed to GameRant, XDA, Gamepur, I

The most common thing for any game developer to do is to make nods to different franchises in their game. Affectionately called easter eggs, these little details are meant to be surprises for players who love various other franchises.

Whether you are a fan of games, television shows, or movies, there’s plenty to discover in Grand Theft Auto 6. Rockstar has taken the time to hide trophies all around Leonida for those who are diligent enough to go searching for them. Even 'Weird Al' Yankovic is hiding in this game somewhere.

Have you found Dexter’s apartment? Maybe you have gone searching for the literal easter egg. Either way, these are amazing nods to other franchises in the mighty GTA 6.

Dexter is a very popular television series about a vigilante serial killer. This serial killer just so happens to live in Miami, which just so happens to be the city that Vice City is based on. What a coincidence!

It just so happens that Rockstar noticed this connection and decided to add a few nods to the series in GTA 6, with the first of them being Dexter’s apartment, which is located on Washington Island.

Of course, you won’t be able to reach this area right away, but once you get access to the map, you can head straight there to check it out and pay tribute to the television series. You can also find some tools on the roof.

Bigfoot has decided to make his way out of the northern forests and headed south to the marshlands of Leonida. It’s a bit weird that he decided to make this island paradise his home, but I won’t question that.

If you progress through the story of GTA 6, there is a mission that will set you on the path to discovering Bigfoot, but he is easy to overlook, as he runs away rather quickly once you get near him.

I barely caught a glimpse of him, but chased him down and caught a photo before he mysteriously disappeared into the marsh. I have no idea if I will see him again, but it was cool to catch a glimpse of this mysterious figure.

After Grand Theft Auto 5's release, the Mount Chiliad mystery was one of the most talked about easter eggs out there. Here's what we know today.

Some developers make nods to different games or pop culture, but Rockstar takes the time to put a literal easter egg in its game. That’s right, if you go to the correct spot on the map, you can find a nest with literal easter eggs in it.

The location in question is a radio tower in South Vice Dale County. The radio tower takes multiple minutes to climb, but the payoff is definitely worth it, as you find a nest full of easter eggs at the top.

While I won’t spoil what happens when you throw one, it does involve a cutscene, and you should definitely take the time to visit the location and watch it unfold.

One of the most memorable missions in GTA 5 is when Michael’s boat gets stolen and you need to chase down the people responsible. Unfortunately, Michael’s boat doesn’t get returned, but we now know the whereabouts of it, and it turns out that it landed in Jason’s yard.

Next to Jason’s house, there is a run-down boat that looks similar to Michael’s. People have pointed this out for a while, but there was no confirmation until now.

Since the game is officially out, you can check out the boat, and you can even climb on top of it. Once there, you can use your phone’s camera to check out the display. Doing so will allow you to see a display that shows part of the GTA 5 map, seemingly confirming that this is Michael’s boat.

Have you ever heard of a movie called Island Claws? If not, you aren’t the only one, but there is a nod to this film in GTA 6. The movie is from the 1980s, and it is about a scientist who genetically modifies crabs to make them grow larger to solve world hunger.

Unfortunately, this doesn’t work out very well, and a similar thing has transpired in Leonida. In Pelican Valley, you can find a trail of blood that leads to a shack. Inside the shack, you can find information about failed experiments on crabs.

Along with this, Jason will get attacked by a giant crab that jumps down on him from the ceiling. It is a rather large easter egg that is meant to pay homage to the classic independent film. Good job doing your research Rockstar.

One of the things that people loved to do in Grand Theft Auto 5 was break into the military base, and the go-to means of breaking into the base was via the hill jump just outside the fence along the highway.

Well, there is another military base in GTA 6 and it is located near where you start the game. It is called Mile Zero Air Station, and it has a convenient hill just outside the fence that you can use to jump into the base just like in GTA 5.

There is no way that this is a coincidence because the hill is an obvious jumping point. Thank you Rockstar for including an obvious homage to the previous game and the silly antics players got into.

From drawings in a mental hospital to TV and radio commercials, these are nine Rockstar Games Easter Eggs that hint at a shared universe.

The most common meme that has ever been spread about a video game is probably the, “We got [insert item here] before GTA 6.” It popped up every single time something major happened before the release of the game. Well it can’t be said anymore since the game is out.

With that being said, the meme will probably become about either Elder Scrolls 6 or GTA 7, or maybe even RAC 7. You might be wondering what that is. Well, if you scroll through your phone’s Snapmatic app, you can find a post about a game called Barnyard Blue: Hot Pursuit.

The post reads, “Barnyard Blue video game before Race and Chase 7 is crazy.” There is even a comment that reads, “We really got Barnyard Blue games before RAC 7.” This is, of course, a jab at the classic meme that appeared for so many years.

The X-Men actually exist in the Grand Theft Auto universe, except they don’t exist in the same way as you know them. Instead of being called the X-Men, they are referred to as the RX-Men. A strange choice, but one that makes it obvious that it is a reference to the classic hero team.

There are various magazines that you can find in the different convenience stores around Leonida, but you can reliably find this magazine in the 24-7 supermarket in northern Key Lento.

In this story, you can find a copy of RX-Men issue 13 titled, “Paging Dr. Placebo.” It features GTA renditions of different heroes and villains. There’s also collectible cards for these characters sprinkled around Leonida.

Speaking of trading cards, you can find various wrestling trading cards across Leonida. This is an obvious easter egg to the various wrestling trading cards that already exist. The wrestling company is called B.A.M, and it stands for Backyard American Mania.

Florida has a rather large wrestling scene, with the WWE training facility being in Orlando. On top of this, various famous wrestlers live or lived in Florida.

To go even further, the acronym of B.A.M. can also be related to Bird Azarian Memorial Training Center, which is located in Illinois, but is a wrestling training center. Odds are, it is just a reference to WWE wrestling.

Early in the game, Jason and Cal sit down to play some games on the JoyStation, with the system itself being an obvious reference to the PlayStation 5. However, that isn’t the whole easter egg, as the game that is played is called Tenebrae Lux.

Tenebrae Lux is a game themed after the movie called The Lighthouse, which features both Robert Pattinson and Willem Dafoe. This is awesome, but there is yet one more easter egg hidden here.

The name Tenebrae Lux is Latin for the term “light-dark.” This happens to be the exact translation of the French term Clair Obscur, which is one of the greatest games to have been released in recent years and a game that took the world by storm.

This list delves into some of the best Easter eggs in video games, exploring how to unlock them, what they're referencing, and more.

AI adoption could disproportionately harm Black and Latino workers

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It has become increasingly clear that the AI revolution is not just poised to upend white collar jobs, despite what public discourse might suggest. While entry level roles are, in fact, on the decline in tech and finance—and employers continue to use AI as a rationale for layoffs—there is ample evidence that rapid AI adoption could interrupt pathways for workers without college degrees. 

The effects of this upheaval will be uneven, which means certain demographic groups are more likely to be impacted. Some of the jobs that have been deemed “highly exposed” to AI, including clerical and administrative roles, are largely dominated by women, and a new report from the McKinsey Institute for Economic Mobility posits that Black and Latino workers could also face major consequences.

The report finds that Black and Latino workers are overrepresented in the sectors where automation and AI adoption will supplant work altogether, rather than augment work. While the overall rate of AI adoption is relatively stable across different demographics, Black and Latino workers will be affected more acutely by job displacement. Across working hours that are impacted by AI adoption, 40% of hours performed by Black workers and 41% by Latino workers will be displaced (as compared to 37% among white workers.) 

That may not seem like a huge gap. But as the report points out, many of the jobs that are most exposed to AI have historically been pathways to the middle class, making them crucial drivers of social mobility across communities that have faced systemic barriers to entry. Black workers, in particular, dominate six jobs that are likely to see the biggest decline in labor demand, according to McKinsey, including postal mail carriers and customer service representatives; Latino workers are also especially likely to hold some of those jobs, namely dishwashers and hand packers. 

Black and Latino workers—along with those in rural regions—are also much more likely to be in jobs that involve physical labor, which are more vulnerable to being automated through robotics and other AI-enabled systems. On the whole, lower wage workers will face the greatest impact and are 7.6 times as likely to need to transition into a new job when compared to higher wage workers; people without a college degree are also 1.8 times more likely to need to do the same. 

Meanwhile, white workers are better positioned to both avoid this level of AI disruption and actually benefit from AI adoption in a number of ways. It’s not just that they’re more likely to hold jobs where AI will likely enhance their work, boosting productivity rather than replacing employees outright. When compared to their Black and Latino counterparts, white workers are also 30-50% more likely to work in the industries where private capital will be deployed, yielding more jobs and wealth creation, the McKinsey report states. 

That means white workers already have more of a foothold in jobs involving robotics or semiconductors or biotech, setting them up for success as hundreds of billions of dollars pour into those industries.(Look no further than the Anthropic IPO this week, which has created thousands of millionaires.)  In other words: Without broader policy changes or reskilling initiatives, many Black and Latino workers may be both forced out of their jobs and locked out of opportunities to cash in on the AI boom.

BigBear.ai vs. Innodata: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One is built on federal and defense contracts, the other on training AI models for big tech, and their financial profiles diverge sharply.

By Sara Appino – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 5:29PM EST

10 stocks we like better than BigBear.ai ›

Choosing between these specialized AI plays requires balancing high-stakes government work against commercial data engineering growth. Investors must decide if BigBear.ai (BBAI -8.67%) or Innodata (INOD -8.91%) better suits their portfolio needs.

BigBear.ai provides complex decision intelligence tools for federal and defense agencies, while Innodata helps major tech firms train generative AI systems. Both companies operate in the rapidly evolving world of artificial intelligence but serve different markets. This comparison examines their financial health, customer concentration, and valuation to help you decide which stock fits your strategy.

BigBear.ai offers decision intelligence solutions that combine data from different sources to help leaders make faster choices. It serves critical sectors like national security, cybersecurity, and global supply chains. According to its latest annual report, a specific contract accounted for nearly 51% of consolidated revenue. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $142.1 million. This represented a revenue growth decline of approximately 1.5% compared to the prior period. The company reported a net loss of roughly $180.2 million during this same time frame.

Following its early 2026 debt conversion, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.0x, meaning the company carries virtually no debt relative to its equity. The current ratio, which measures the ability to pay short-term debts with short-term assets, is close to 1.8x. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was negative at nearly $46.3 million.

Innodata operates as a global data engineering firm that prepares and evaluates data for large-scale AI systems. The company serves several high-profile technology leaders, with one customer in its digital data solutions segment accounting for nearly 58% of total revenue in 2025. This focus on the best small cap tech stocks in the data sector highlights its role in the generative AI boom.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $350.2 million. This reflects a revenue growth increase of nearly 46.9% over the previous year. The company also achieved a net income of roughly $48.5 million during this period.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio is approximately 2.7x. The debt-to-equity ratio is 0.0x, indicating a strong balance sheet with no total debt. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was nearly $35.6 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 23.8% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

BigBear.ai faces significant risk due to heavy revenue concentration with government customers who can terminate contracts for convenience. Scaling its sales efforts remains a challenge while managing long and unpredictable sales cycles. It also faces intense competition from Microsoft (MSFT -2.77%) and Amazon (AMZN -4.10%). The company also navigates complex AI deployment risks and recent financial volatility including litigation over accounting errors.

Innodata relies heavily on a single customer that contributed nearly 58% of its 2025 revenues, making it vulnerable to contract changes. It competes against well-resourced firms like Accenture (ACN +1.19%) and Cognizant Technology Solutions (CTSH +3.11%) in the data engineering space. The business also deals with geopolitical risks from its global workforce and ongoing legal challenges regarding securities class actions.

Innodata appears to offer a more attractive value proposition based on its lower P/S ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with Innodata. AI models are only as good as the data they learn from, and Innodata helps big tech companies create and test that data. Its sales have grown year over year for 13 straight quarters, and it makes money.

Last quarter, sales jumped 53% and management raised its growth outlook, though the stock has fallen sharply this fall. There are risks: Two customers make up most of its sales, margins narrowed, and one program is winding down. But Innodata is adding new customers and work from a large cloud company, and expects to replace the lost program by early next year.

To give BigBear.ai its due, it does high-stakes work for national security and border security customers with contracts that are difficult to replace, and its backlog is growing. But it's still losing money, and government contract sales can be difficult to predict. That uncertainty is where Innodata stands apart: It is already profitable, with plenty of cash to keep investing as demand for AI data grows.

Texas Democrats propose bills to nix state DEI ban at public colleges

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The party clinched the state’s House and governorship in the midterm elections, though that still may not be enough to get the legislation passed.

In the state’s House, Reps. Nicole Collier and John Bucy III have put forward bills that would repeal the 2023 law. Texas state Sen. José Menéndez introduced a companion bill in the state’s Senate.

At any other time in the past three decades, the new bills might have faced little chance of passage simply by virtue of being championed by Democrats. But the party now controls two-thirds of a trifecta for the first time since 1996 — a makeup that may still not be enough to pass the legislation.

Senate Bill 17 — the target of the new bills — bans several DEI activities at public colleges, including:

Texas was one of the first states to ban DEI offices and initiatives in higher education. Following SB 17 and a similar law in Florida, universities from Ohio to Iowa to Alaska shuttered their DEI offices, often under legislative mandates.

Institutions quickly responded to SB 17. For instance, the University of North Texas said before the law took effect that it would restructure its Multicultural Center and Pride Alliance into the student affairs office and launch a new Center for Belonging and Engagement.

After passage, the Texas A&M University System issued guidance stating that DEI terminology couldn’t be used on the institution’s websites. Offices dedicated to student affinity communities would have to close, though it said some programs and libraries could be absorbed into broader offices and initiatives.

Texas Tech University also closed its DEI office.

Texas Tech University System’s latest chancellor — Brandon Creighton,  who helped author SB 17 — has gone even further than the bill’s requirements, which don’t cover academic content. The system has moved to eliminate academic programs focused on sexual orientation and gender identity and put restrictions on teaching about race, sex and gender in courses.

State audits so far have determined that the University of Texas System, nearly all of the institutions within theTexas A&M University System and 19 of the state’s community colleges are compliant with SB 17.

Audits last year deemed two institutions noncompliant — a community college because it made a new employee participate in a DEI training and Texas A&M-Central Texas because it struck a contract that didn’t conform to the law’s ban.

Colleges would be on the hook for up to $100,000 in fees for each international student seeking to participate in Optional Practical Training.

The family foundation of Cintas Corp. founder Richard Farmer pledged up to $100 million to Miami University’s business school over the next decade.

SpaceX's Starshield troubles in Ukraine could threaten its broader military business

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“Explain the changes that were made to the Starshield service in October 2026.”

The representative in the US House expected to chair the House Intelligence Committee in January, Jim Himes (D-Conn.), has threatened to withhold support for funding government-owned systems from SpaceX amid the ongoing inquiry into the use of Starshield in Ukraine.

“SpaceX’s potentially unilateral modification of a U.S. Government contract, combined with its lack of responsiveness to congressional oversight, calls into question every contract the U.S. Government has with SpaceX,” Himes wrote in a letter to SpaceX founder Elon Musk on Friday.

CNN first reported the letter’s existence. The network also reported that Himes sent a classified communication to Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton with a similar message that it would be difficult for Congress to authorize funding for systems with SpaceX as the primary vendor.

SpaceX has developed the consumer Starlink service over the last decade, which provides high-speed Internet from low-Earth orbit to user terminals on Earth. For government customers, SpaceX has also developed Starshield, a suite of satellite services that includes a militarized version of Starlink communications. The US government has purchased Starshield services and extended access to Ukraine, although the terms governing that access are largely classified.

During the last several weeks, a controversy has erupted over interruptions to Starshield service in parts of Ukraine, cutting off connectivity to drones used to defend against Russian attacks.

On October 19, for example, Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said of Starshield on social media, “Unfortunately, we have established that the equipment stops functioning at speeds of 130–161 kilometers per hour in various sectors of the front. This is slowing down our frontline logistics and limiting the use of various types of drones, particularly mid-strike drones and interceptors of jet-powered ‘shaheds.’” Additional disruptions were reported a week ago.

The central question is who decided to curtail the performance of Starshield assets in Ukraine. Accounts have variously implicated Musk, SpaceX, the Pentagon, and the White House. Musk has said that US officials control Starshield access, while defending SpaceX’s right to restrict the use of commercial Starlink service in war.

“My ask to you is straightforward,” Himes wrote to Musk. “Explain the changes that were made to the Starshield service in October 2026, why those changes were made, and whether those changes were made unilaterally by SpaceX or were directed by a U.S. Government official.”

The communications from Himes suggest that Congressional Democrats, who will control the House and Senate in January following decisive victories in the Midterm elections, are prepared to not just investigate the disruption of these SpaceX services in Ukraine, but to take action that could threaten SpaceX’s broader national security business.

“It is difficult for me to continue supporting the authorization of funding for these SpaceX-developed, government-owned systems, until I receive satisfactory answers to my questions about the Starshield service in Ukraine,” Himes wrote in his letter to Musk.

Various options are open to Congressional Democrats, as well as some Republicans who have raised similar concerns, including demanding classified briefings and documents and restricting funding through appropriations to the US Department of Defense and other federal departments. Notably, the US Space Force awarded SpaceX a $2.29 billion contract earlier this year to develop the Space Data Network Backbone intended to support the Golden Dome missile defense initiative. That program, along with SpaceX’s work building intelligence satellites for the National Reconnaissance Office, could become a focus of congressional scrutiny.

It seems unlikely that the US government would sever ties with SpaceX for space-based data services or launches, as the company is currently providing capabilities that no other US company can match. But Democrats are clearly serious about putting restrictions on Musk’s ability to influence geopolitical conflicts that have traditionally been the purview of governments rather than corporations.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

New York’s Luxury Rental Market Sees Influx of Cash from AI Boom

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The artificial intelligence boom has expanded from the rolling hills of San Francisco and Silicon Valley to the busy streets of New York City, where an already tight luxury market is feeling the impact.

The AI wealth boom was in the spotlight this week, following Anthropic’s highly anticipated IPO and jaw dropping $1.494 trillion valuation. With the tech constantly growing and producing newly minted millionaires, the impacts of deep-pocketed AI professionals entering the New York real estate market are just beginning to be felt, agents there say.

There are more than 2,000 AI companies operating in New York City, according to the city’s Economic Development Corporation, and AI companies signed leases for about 1.5 million square feet in just the first six months of 2026, the Brooklyn Daily Eagle reported.

Major AI frontrunners like Anthropic, Palantir, OpenAI, and EliseAI have opened offices in New York, contributing to the thriving commercial real estate market.

“It’s been pretty unbelievable, what’s happening in the public sphere of the AI business. We’re seeing a ton of AI leasing activity on the ground in New York,” Evan Kleinberg, co-founder of Cornelia Street Partners, a boutique commercial real estate firm, told Realtor.com. “And it’s been happening so fast, it’s almost representative of the technology itself. The companies hire immediately and they need space yesterday.”

While Kleinberg has been keeping eyes on the AI industry for the past couple of years, his brokerage worked with its first AI specific company about 12 months ago, at the end of 2025. Since then, the brokerage has secured leases for five companies developing proprietary AI models.

There are a number of reasons Kleinberg sees AI companies expanding east and planting roots in New York. The city has fully rebounded from the pandemic and has an attractive lifestyle that draws many young professionals. Plus, companies see the benefit of being close to their portfolio businesses.

“Ultimately, if your goal is to raise money from Goldman Sachs or JP Morgan or one of the major private equity groups like Apollo, they’re all here,” Kleinberg explained. “So wanting to get close to them is an underlying reason to move.”

With these new offices comes a hoard of young, newly wealthy professionals who are looking to build a home in New York, many of whom are stepping into an already fiercely competitive luxury real estate market.

“Wall Street has historically been a major driver of Manhattan luxury real estate, and now we’re really starting to see the effects of AI. It’s becoming an important engine for wealth creation and housing demand,” Michelle Griffith, a residential real estate agent in New York City, told Realtor.com. “Right now in Manhattan, we are already in a very tight rental market. Even for the last year, rents are up anywhere from 10% to 20% in Manhattan, especially for these luxury properties.”

As of November, the median sold price of a home in Manhattan is $1,150,000, a 15% year over year increase. Some Manhattan neighborhoods have had more extreme changes.

The Lower East Side, for example, saw the median sale price jump 28% year over year to $1,435,000. Rent in the Lower East Side also increased 11% year over year, for a median of $5,947 per month.

The New York City luxury residential landscape is no stranger to competition, with deep pocketed masses vying for a severely limited inventory of spaces. With the flood of AI professionals, another layer of people has been added to the market.

Griffith recalled a recent listing at 20 Gramercy Park North, which had historically rented for $30,000. While a rental like this, which is in the higher end of the market, would previously take 30 to 60 days to identify a tenant, the listing had eight rental bids after 48 hours on the market, with half above asking.

Most workers are determined to live close to work, making it especially challenging to secure an apartment in these highly sought after neighborhoods.

OpenAI’s 90,000-square-foot New York office is located in the Puck Building, which sits on the boarder of SoHo and Nolita in Manhattan.

Anthropic’s New York hub takes up the entirety of a 16-story building at 330 Hudson Street in the Hudson Square neighborhood of Lower Manhattan.

“The offices are in fantastic locations, so it’s easy to find great housing within walking distance,” said Griffith. “The challenge is finding that apartment because there is very, very limited inventory because those neighborhoods were already popular and in demand.”

For luxury agents, the challenge is no longer finding tenants with the money to rent high priced rentals, its finding the rentals for deep pocketed tenants.

When Griffith began in real estate 15 years ago, an apartment for $100,000 per month might be the biggest rental of the year. Now, rentals for $80,000 to even $150,000 are common and moving quickly, she shared.

One hand on the controller, one on the pizza roll: How America fed itself through GTA VI’s $3.79 billion launch night

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The biggest video game launch in years left millions of players with a practical problem: how to eat without putting down the controller.

Rockstar Games, a unit of Take-Two Interactive, said the game brought in an estimated $3.79 billion in retail sales worldwide on its first day, which it called the biggest entertainment launch in history. With that demand, there are surely some tired people holding onto those controllers but hungry for a snack.

It seems like the delivery apps already planned for it. Uber Eats picked several of its Grand Theft Auto VI launch-day deals for food that could be eaten with one hand. In the U.S., the list included buy-one-get-one McNuggets at McDonald’s, boneless wings at Wingstop, pizza at Papa John’s and Whopper Jr. burgers at Burger King.

“We even picked some of our food deals with one-handed eating in mind, so fans can keep their focus on the game,” Uber President and COO Andrew Macdonald said in a statement to Fortune.

Gamers ordered accordingly. Between midnight and 5 a.m. on launch night, Uber Eats orders in Miami, the real city behind the game’s Vice City, rose more than 30% from the prior two Thursdays, Macdonald said on X. Nationwide, orders of Red Bull Watermelon rose 95%, Totino’s Pizza Rolls 54% and Ghost Sour Strips 46%. Uber told Fortune it had no separate data on one-handed food orders.

DoorDash reported a similar pattern. Between 9 p.m. and 2 a.m. ET, orders for chips, burgers, fries and pizza each rose more than 10% compared with the average of the six prior Wednesdays, the company told Fortune. Energy drinks rose more than 20%. Miami led all major markets, with late-night orders up nearly 25%, and Philadelphia, Detroit and Atlanta also saw double-digit increases, according to DoorDash’s data dashboard. DoorDash said it could not compare the food increases with its overall order growth.

Some of the biggest jumps were for gear, not food. Orders for controllers and gaming accessories rose 450% on DoorDash, HDMI cables 440% and headsets 70%. Searches for GTA VI on the app rose more than 940% in the week before launch.

“GTA 6 is more than a game launch. It’s a cultural moment that fans have been waiting years for,” Gina Igwe, DoorDash’s vice president of brand, creative and consumer marketing, said in a statement to Fortune.

Players who weren’t playing were watching. In its first two days, GTA VI drew more livestream watch time than any game has in its entire launch week, more than Elden Ring, Hogwarts Legacy and Cyberpunk 2077 drew in their first weeks combined, according to Streams Charts.

Total Twitch watch time rose 61% compared with the two days before launch, the firm said, while World of Warcraft and Counter-Strike lost about 31% of their Twitch watch time and Fortnite fell 60% on YouTube. Kick’s “Just Sleeping” category rose 176% as streamers ran round-the-clock GTA VI marathons.

Restaurant chains also moved to get a share. Popeyes is selling a Spice City sandwich, named after the game’s setting, through Nov. 22 and told players to order it through its app “between missions.” McDonald’s began taking delivery orders on Discord, the chat app many gamers use while they play. DoorDash ran offers with Wendy’s and the Cheesecake Factory, and Grubhub offered $10 off to its first 1,000 customers.

Analytics firm Sensor Tower expects the game to drive more than 50 million additional visits to U.S. food delivery and restaurant apps in its first 30 days, Reuters reported.

Neither company put a dollar figure on launch night. A rough Fortune estimate suggests the extra takeout spending was small next to the game itself. Uber and DoorDash together handled about $680 million in delivery orders a day worldwide in the third quarter, based on their earnings reports. If late-night hours make up roughly a tenth of that, and launch night lifted those orders 10% to 25%, the extra spending comes to roughly $7 million to $17 million. That’s about 1% to 2.5% of a normal day’s delivery business for the two companies, and less than half a percent of the $3.79 billion Rockstar says the game brought in on day one.

That shift fits what economists say about events like this one. Much of the money moves from one kind of spending to another rather than appearing from nowhere. “People just rearrange how they’re spending their free time,” Victor Matheson, an economist at the College of the Holy Cross, told Fortune before the launch.

Restaurants also had fewer hands. Missed restaurant shifts rose 92% week over week on Nov. 19, according to data from scheduling platform Harri shared with Fortune, covering 280,000 shifts. One in 14 shifts went unworked, compared with one in 27 the Thursday before, while sales at those restaurants fell less than 2%.

Grand Theft Auto VI came out Nov. 19 on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X|S, more than 13 years after its predecessor. Before the launch, a widely shared estimate claimed workers skipping their jobs to play would cost the U.S. economy $1 billion, a figure Matheson told Fortune he doesn’t believe. Office attendance barely moved. Wi-Fi logins at the more than 250 U.S. locations of Industrious, a coworking company owned by CBRE, fell about 5% on launch day compared with the previous three Thursdays, the company told Fortune.

Catherina covers markets, the economy, energy, tech, and AI.

Stock Market Today, Nov. 20: Stocks Plunge as AI Trade Unwinds

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Wall Street's worst stretch of the year deepened as AI-linked names sold off while defensive sectors drew buyers, today, Nov. 20, 2026.

By Emma Newbery – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

Falling 3.70% to 6,910, the S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) entered a technical correction after a seven-day losing streak, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -5.28%) dropped 5.28% to 22,618, erasing its annual gains. The Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -1.93%) fell 1.93% to 48,147.

Gold prices fell 3.02% to $3,517.24 as of U.S. market close, and the 10-Year Treasury yield slipped 0.10% to 4.91%. Sector-wise, energy and utility stocks gained, while technology and industrials tumbled.

Nvidia Corp (NVDA -14.01%) plummeted 14.01% following reports of a potential SEC accounting probe that accelerated a broader unwind of artificial intelligence trades. Broadcom Inc (AVGO -12.19%) tumbled 12.26% on unconfirmed reports that Alphabet's (GOOG -2.59%) Google would cut next year's chip shipments. Conversely, UnitedHealth Group Inc (UNH +2.83%)rose after TD Cowen upgraded the stock.

The sell-off in artificial intelligence (AI) stocks accelerated today, as simmering nerves boiled over into widespread loss of confidence. Nvidia's deepening woes only magnified the fallout. The Nasdaq Composite has fallen more than 10% this week, and the S&P 500 officially entered a technical correction after dropping 10% below its record high.

Institutional capital is rotating into defensive sectors, including retail and consumer staples. Walmart Inc (WMT +1.51%)gained after raising its full-year forecast in yesterday's earnings. Investors will be watching holiday grocery spending for signs of consumer resilience.

For investors, these dramatic shifts can be scary. It is important to remember that markets have always recovered, even from serious sell-offs, and that in the long term, what matters is staying invested. Holding a diversified portfolio of quality stocks for at least five years is a proven way to build wealth.

The Internet Archive's workers are unionizing

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

They asked founder Brewster Kahle to voluntarily recognize the union, but will go to an NLRB vote if needed.

Workers at the Internet Archive, home to the Wayback Machine, are unionizing. After signing up to join the Communications Workers of America (CWA), they're asking founder Brewster Kahle to voluntarily recognize the union. As a backup, they've filed for a National Labor Relations Board (NLRB) election. Kahle hasn't responded publicly yet.

Wired reports that over 80 of 124 eligible US workers signed on. None of their asks are radical: better pay, additional benefits that apply to everyone and paths for career development and promotion. Organizing committee member Danielle Leard tied the unionization campaign to the Internet Archive's mission, stating that job security, benefits and livable wages go "hand-in-hand with sustaining the Archive long-term."

There's a recent precedent from another Bay Area nonprofit. Wikimedia Foundation workers joined the CWA in September, but only after the foundation refused to voluntarily recognize the effort and it went to an NLRB vote. Wikimedia workers ultimately voted in favor by a count of 158 to 14.

The Internet Archive has had a rocky few years. In 2023, a judge ruled that it violated copyright law by lending ebooks, and it suffered a major cyberattack the following year that left the Wayback Machine out of commission for weeks. Adding to the troubles, many news outlets began blocking the Archive's crawlers, fearing its archival copies could be used as a workaround for AI scraping. But the news hasn't been all bad: the US Senate designated the Internet Archive as an official US government document depository in 2025.

‘You Can See Everything’: Elizabeth Holmes Documentary Crossing $100M Worldwide Tonight

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A24‘s Lance Oppenheim and Nathan Fielder co-directed groundbreaking documentary, You Can See Everything, continues to have 20/20 vision at the box office and will pass the $100M global mark tonight.

Broken out that’s $90.9M at the domestic B.O. with a foreign cume that’s coursing to $8M today. Already, the movie is the second highest grossing non-concert documentary at the domestic B.O. after Michael Moore’s Cannes Palme d’Or winning Fahrenheit 9/11 which counts $119.1M.

Already, You Can See Everything counts $6.4M abroad having already unspooled in Turkey (Oct. 30, $458K), Poland ($1.46M), Czech Republic ($163K) and Australia/New Zealand ($2.5M). Today the movie is clearing $2M offshore inclusive of $1.2M in the UK/Ireland (which also includes Thursday monies). A24 has the movie in the UK. Watch on Deadline Related Stories Casting Louis Garrel And Harry Lawtey Join Jeremy Allen White In Ben Stiller-Directed 'Airman' From Laika Live-Action And A24 News 'Primetime' Cinematographer & Editor On Making Robert Pattinson Look Like A "Superhero" & Breakdown Viral Meme Catchphrase

The pic is also hitting Russia and CIS (via VLG) and Philippines, Scandinavia, Middle East, and South Africa (via Sony) today. As Deadline first reported, Sony Pictures acquired rights to the documentary in Germany/Austria, Latin America, Scandinavia, Greece, the Middle East, Israel, India, South Africa, South Korea, and Southeast Asia.

Abroad the Elizabeth Holmes and Billy Evans sit-down is setting market records in some territories as the widest-ever theatrical release for a documentary. In UK/Ireland, You Can See Everything is playing in 600 cinemas; that location count being the widest ever for a non-concert documentary. You Can See Everything also posted the biggest presales in A24 UK history. Screenings there sold out a month in advance of its release with theaters adding extra showtimes due to demand (including midnight shows and extended operating hours). There’s been a big turnout by Gen Z and young millennial audiences, which is unusual for documentaries in recent years, outside of concert films. Cineworld, which has 87 sites across the UK reported that a third of advance ticket sales are coming from 18–24 year olds, with about two thirds from the under 35 demo. Picturehouse has currently sold more tickets to the documentary than No Time to Die and Star Wars: The Rise of Skywalker at the same point in time before release. Everyman had sold more tickets at eight days out than Deadpool & Wolverine at the same point. At Curzon, only No Time to Die had stronger pre-sales at this stage. Lighthouse Dublin broke its all time presale record with You Can See Everything, ahead of Barbie.

Among the Sony market holdovers, Portugal posted the 2nd highest per screen average in the weekend’s top 10, while the pic bowed to No. 1 in the Baltics, No. 2 in Estonia, and No. 2 in Lithuania and Latvia (behind a local title). In Turkey, You Can See Everything has officially sold the most tickets of any international documentary ever in Turkey and reps the third highest opening for A24 behind only Backrooms and The Drama. Last weekend, You Can See Everything opened to $2.5M in Australia/New Zealand for the No. 1 spot, which is the highest start for a non-concert doc in the territory and third highest when including concerts (Michael Jackson’s This Is It and Taylor Swift’s Eras Tour rank ahead). It’s also the third highest opening for an A24 title in the territory.

Meantime, the global marketing for You Can See Everything remains the meme that doesn’t stop giving. AI has taken full control, which you can see below; a possible topic for another Oppenheim and Fielder documentary down the road in these Anthropic and OpenAI times.

Here’s the rest of the international release schedule for You Can See Everything:

Nov 19 & 20: UK, Russia & CIS, Scandi, UAE, Saudi Arabia, Kuwait, Qatar, Bahrain, Iraq, Oman, Greece, South Africa, Philippines.Nov. 26 & 27: Mexico, Brazil, Argentina, Italy (event screenings), Slovakia, Slovenia, Egypt, Jordan, Lebanon, Singapore, Israel, Romania, Vietnam, India, Taiwan, Indonesia, Bulgaria.Dec. 3 & 4: Germany, Austria, Switzerland, Hong Kong, Thailand, Malaysia, Chile, Peru.
