EU, U.K. Agree to a Brexit Reset

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: EU, U.K. Agree to a Brexit Reset

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at joint EU-U.K. efforts to reverse Brexit, Russia’s latest incursions into Poland, and the future of Myanmar’s Rohingya population.

Ten years after the United Kingdom voted to leave the European Union, Brexit is back on the table. This time, though, London appears eager to reverse some of the barriers created by Brexit and bridge the decade-long divide between Britain and the bloc.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at joint EU-U.K. efforts to reverse Brexit, Russia’s latest incursions into Poland, and the future of Myanmar’s Rohingya population.

Ten years after the United Kingdom voted to leave the European Union, Brexit is back on the table. This time, though, London appears eager to reverse some of the barriers created by Brexit and bridge the decade-long divide between Britain and the bloc.

At a highly anticipated EU-U.K. summit in Brussels on Friday, British and EU leaders unveiled a modest deal to begin resetting the relationship. Under the agreement, the EU and United Kingdom will realign their carbon emissions trading systems; relax restrictions on food and agricultural trade—a major win for British farmers who have long complained about tedious export paperwork; and kick-start a “youth experience” program focused on travel, study, and work.

Although these are minor changes compared to the breadth of the 2016 Brexit framework, British Prime Minister Andy Burnham said on Friday that the deal could inject more than $22 billion into the U.K. economy in what he called the “single biggest growth measure we could take in this Parliament.”

“Today we draw the line on a decade of drift and division by reconnecting with old friends, by building a much more constructive relationship with our nearest neighbors and our largest market,” Burnham said.

According to a YouGov poll published in June, a majority of Britons believe that leaving the EU was a mistake and that London should move toward rejoining the bloc. Burnham has capitalized on this discontent, ignoring warnings from Conservative and Reform UK lawmakers who are against reopening the Brexit debate.

“It’s like the emperor’s new clothes—where no one is prepared to say the obvious truth,” Burnham said on Monday ahead of the summit. “We have lost more than we have gained in the last 10 years.” A National Bureau of Economic Research working paper found that by the end of 2025, Brexit had cut British GDP by 6 percent to 8 percent and investment by 12 percent to 13 percent.

Analysts suggest that global insecurity fueled by Russia’s war in Ukraine, the U.S. trade war, and energy market unpredictability have further convinced Britons that a post-Brexit world comes with greater instability.

“Our security and defence partnership, as well as our united support for Ukraine, are central to our commitment to address together the growing geopolitical challenges we face,” European Council President António Costa wrote on X on Friday, adding that the “EU and the UK are both stronger when we work together.”

It is unclear how much closer the bridging of ties will go. For every step toward dismantling Brexit, Burnham is expected to face backlash back home. “This is a total betrayal of Brexit Britain,” far-right Reform UK leader Niger Farage, a chief architect of the 2016 vote, said last month after Burnham floated restoring freedom of movement with the EU.

On Brussels’s side, though, the EU remains open to having “the closest possible relationship” with the United Kingdom, Costa said.

Missile incursions. Two Russian cruise missiles and a suspected drone entered Poland on Friday, adding to the growing number of reported Russian incursions into NATO territory in recent months. The incidents occurred while Russia was carrying out a massive overnight bombardment on Ukraine.

The intrusions coincided with European military exercises in Poland that began on Nov. 9; one Russian missile reportedly crashed only 22 miles from where the drills were taking place. No injuries were reported; however, Polish Defense Minister Wladyslaw Kosiniak-Kamysz said it was “not accidental” that Russia’s incursions and the European military drills occurred in tandem. “We are dealing with an intentional provocation” by Moscow, Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk added. Moscow has yet to comment.

According to Ukrainian Deputy Energy Minister Artem Nekrasov, Russia’s nighttime barrage also triggered emergency outages across 11 Ukrainian regions as strikes on critical energy infrastructure forced three nuclear power plants to reduce generation. Russian attacks on Friday also reportedly hit apartment buildings, medical facilities, and bridges. Moscow’s Defense Ministry said it targeted sites where weapons were being manufactured.

Genocide ruling. Myanmar’s military-run government on Friday denounced an International Court of Justice (ICJ) ruling that found the country’s security forces guilty of genocide against its minority Rohingya Muslim community. The landmark decision, issued on Thursday, marked the first time that the court has found a country to be directly responsible for the crime.

“The court finds that Myanmar’s security forces killed thousands of Rohingya civilians, perpetrated widespread acts of sexual violence, as well as acts of extreme violence against children, and attacked fleeing Rohingya as they sought to flee in large numbers,” ICJ President Yuji Iwasawa said during a readout of the 9-6 ruling.

Myanmar’s Foreign Ministry called the decision a “great disappointment” and noted that the judgment was not unanimous. It also said that the roughly 1.3 million Rohingya refugees in neighboring Bangladesh will only be allowed to return “when situations permit.”

However, Tom Andrews, the former U.N. special rapporteur on human rights in Myanmar, told Foreign Policy that a 9-6 result is still a “strong majority.” But he said that the international community must take additional steps to ensure that the country’s ruling junta faces real repercussions for its actions. “Because the thing is, if we establish that there is no consequence for genocide, then we can be guaranteed that genocide is going to happen again,” Andrews said. “And it’s only by imposing consequences that we can begin to see justice prevail, not only in this case but in future cases.”

Resetting relations. Chinese President Xi Jinping celebrated a “comprehensive turnaround” in his country’s relationship with Australia on Friday. Speaking on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit, Xi said that Beijing is eager to set aside the two nations’ differences and expand cooperation in several critical sectors, such as artificial intelligence, energy, agriculture, and mining.

China is Australia’s top trading partner. However, ties between the Indo-Pacific powers began to deteriorate in 2020 after Canberra called for an inquiry into the origins of the COVID-19 pandemic, prompting China to impose punitive tariffs on Australian goods. In recent years, though, Australian Prime Minister Anthony Albanese has sought to restore bilateral relations, especially as the future of his country’s critical iron ore industry comes under threat.

For decades, Chinese iron ore purchases have invigorated Australia’s economy; the vital resource was exempt from China’s 2020 duties. But with Beijing diversifying its partnerships in Africa and Latin America, where iron ore can be supplied more cheaply, as well as exercising increased leverage over iron ore pricing, Canberra is worried that its export earnings will take a major hit.

On Wednesday, Iranian forces targeted a U.S. air base located in which country?

While Trump can brag about Xi meeting him on the tarmac in Shenzhen, China, this week, Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum has something she can brag about from her trip, too: going viral on Chinese social media. After spending the roughly 21-hour trip from Mexico City to Shenzhen (with a stopover in Tijuana, Mexico) sitting in a middle seat on an economy flight, Sheinbaum was greeted at the airport by a military guard and a dancing troupe. But they didn’t meet her at her plane—instead, she arrived to the welcome ceremony via airport bus.

Her down-to-earth travel style seems to have appealed to many Chinese netizens: A hashtag about her arrival was seen 10 million times on the Chinese social media platform Weibo, according to Bloomberg.

The tit-for-tat strikes between Iran and the United States this week marked an end to a roughly two-month lull in such attacks, FP’s John Haltiwanger and Rishi Iyengar write in Situation Report.

Microsoft ‘To Do’ users make a big to-do over popular task app’s pending retirement

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 20, 2026 at 2:06 pmNovember 20, 2026 at 2:07 pm

Microsoft acquired the popular Wunderlist app in 2015, shut it down in 2020 and told its loyal users that Microsoft To Do would be their new home. Now they’re being asked to pack up again.

Microsoft plans to retire the To Do apps on June 30, 2027, moving personal task management for work and school accounts into Planner, its team project-management app. Microsoft disclosed the change Wednesday in a long Planner blog post amid the company’s Ignite announcements, and Windows Latest flagged the change later that day.

The reaction was swift and mostly unhappy. Tom Warren of The Verge summed it up on X: “Microsoft is killing its great tasks app in favor of its worse tasks app.”

That’s not entirely fair to Planner, which is built for a different job. But it captures the gripe: To Do was simple, free and worked across Windows, Mac, iOS, Android and the web, and nothing Microsoft is offering replaces all of that.

For work and school accounts, Microsoft says Planner “will be the destination for managing personal work tasks,” but “existing To Do tasks and lists will not migrate to Planner.” It says it will offer a way to export To Do data before the app shuts down.

Tony Redmond of Office365itpros, who tracks Microsoft 365 closely, cataloged the gaps: no file attachments on private Planner tasks, no equivalent for To Do’s list groups, no transfer of shared-list membership, assignments or permissions, and no native Planner apps for Windows or Mac.

He also noted the irony that Outlook’s old Exchange-based tasks will outlive To Do.

Why Microsoft is doing it: The company said putting individual and shared work on the same foundation lets it deliver more consistent features, and lets people connect their own tasks to Copilot and other AI agents. A new Planner agent, available with a Copilot add-on license, can take on tasks and generate status reports.

The Register’s Richard Speed put it less charitably, saying Microsoft had slipped the news into “this week’s Ignite AI puffery.”

Users weigh in: Brad Silverberg, the former Microsoft executive who led Windows 95 development and later became a Seattle venture investor, posted on X that he has used To Do daily, and Wunderlist before it.

“Very disappointed now,” he wrote, adding that he’s looking at TickTick and Todoist.

Similar posts piled up on LinkedIn from Microsoft 365 consultants and everyday users, some of them describing Planner as clunky by comparison.

Patrick Husting, a Seattle-area entrepreneur and software developer whose Ghostwriter add-in brought AI to Microsoft Office before Microsoft announced Microsoft 365 Copilot, offered on LinkedIn to maintain To Do for Microsoft for free.

Rivals smell an opportunity: Competitors wasted no time. TickTick posted a migration guide for To Do refugees. Todoist CEO Amir Salihefendic wrote on LinkedIn that the shutdown was “pretty unexpected” and pointed out that his app has outlasted competitors for 20 years.

The most pointed pitch came from Superlist, which called itself “the natural successor” and said it already has a tool for importing To Do lists. Wunderlist founder Christian Reber started the company in 2020.

Reber and his co-founders sold Wunderlist’s parent company, 6Wunderkinder, to Microsoft in 2015 for a reported $100 million to $200 million. In 2019, as Microsoft prepared to shut the app down, he publicly asked Satya Nadella to sell it back. Microsoft kept it, then retired it in favor of To Do.

Now some of those former Wunderlist users may end up back with the people who built it.

5 Evidence-Based Treatments for Borderline Personality Disorder

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 20, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

For a long time, borderline personality disorder (BPD) was considered one of the most difficult mental health conditions to treat. Some therapists even believed that people with BPD couldn't get better.

Fortunately, decades of research have demonstrated otherwise. Today, several treatments can help people with BPD manage intense emotions, build healthier relationships, and reduce impulsive behaviors.

Dialectical Behavior Therapy (DBT) is probably the best known, but it's not the only option. Here are five approaches worth considering if you're looking for care for yourself or a loved one.

Developed by psychologist Marsha Linehan, DBT was the first treatment for BPD to demonstrate effectiveness in a randomized clinical trial.

The results were incredibly promising. Among chronically suicidal women with BPD, those receiving DBT engaged in less self-harm, had fewer psychiatric hospitalizations, and were more likely to stay in treatment than those receiving usual care.

Since then, DBT has been evaluated in dozens of randomized clinical trials. Its strongest evidence is for reducing suicidal and self-injurious behaviors, although promising findings extend to substance use, eating disorders, and depression.

DBT is based on the idea that people with BPD experience emotions intensely and may rely on coping strategies that provide temporary relief but create problems in the long run.

According to the treatment manual, comprehensive (full model) DBT programs include several components: weekly individual therapy, weekly skills group, and between-session phone coaching. In individual therapy, it is common to review difficulties that came up during the week and explore alternative responses. In skills group, patients receive didactic (class-like) instruction in four skill areas: mindfulness, emotion regulation, distress tolerance, and interpersonal effectiveness. Between-session phone coaching helps people apply these skills when they need them most.

Initially, Marsha Linehan recommended that people with BPD make a year-long commitment to their DBT program. However, many DBT programs now offer care for shorter durations (e.g., 6 months).

Developed by psychologist Peter Fonagy and psychiatrist Anthony Bateman, MBT has demonstrated improvements in BPD symptoms, self-harm, and interpersonal functioning in randomized clinical trials.

MBT focuses on improving mentalizing, our ability to understand our own thoughts and feelings and those of others. Rather than explicitly teaching coping skills, as in DBT, therapists help patients strengthen this ability through interactions during therapy.

For example, imagine leaving a session convinced your therapist was annoyed with you. Rather than simply reassuring you, an MBT therapist might explore what happened and what each of you might have been thinking or feeling.

The therapeutic relationship becomes a place to practice navigating emotionally charged interactions. Treatment typically includes weekly individual and group sessions over 12–18 months.

Developed by psychiatrist Otto Kernberg and colleagues, TFP has demonstrated improvements in BPD symptoms and interpersonal functioning in randomized clinical trials.

Like MBT, TFP focuses on relationships rather than teaching specific coping skills. However, TFP emphasizes difficulties integrating positive and negative perceptions of ourselves and others.

For example, someone with BPD might see their partner as loving and trustworthy one moment, but cruel and uncaring after a disagreement. TFP helps patients recognize that positive and negative qualities can exist in the same person.

Treatment typically involves twice-weekly individual sessions lasting a year or longer.

Developed by psychiatrist John Gunderson, GPM takes a different approach; the goal is to make effective BPD treatment accessible through generalist mental health clinicians.

Rather than requiring extensive training in specialized psychotherapy, GPM provides a practical framework for understanding BPD, managing crises, and improving everyday functioning.

Particular attention is given to relationships, work, school, and other meaningful activities. Treatment typically involves weekly appointments, with flexibility based on patients' needs.

Importantly, a randomized clinical trial comparing general psychiatric management with DBT found substantial improvements in both groups, including reductions in suicidal and self-injurious behaviors.

This work challenged the assumption that effective BPD treatment necessarily requires a highly specialized, intensive program.

BPD Compass is a newer cognitive-behavioral treatment my colleagues and I developed to make evidence-based BPD care more efficient and accessible.

We were intrigued that existing treatments all produce meaningful improvements, with no single approach consistently outperforming the others. This suggests that each addresses something important, whether that's emotion regulation (DBT), relationships (MBT, TFP), or building a meaningful life (GPM).

Yet many patients continue experiencing symptoms after treatment. We wondered whether nonresponse might reflect a mismatch between patients' difficulties and the treatment they receive.

We designed BPD Compass to address three broad vulnerabilities associated with BPD: emotion dysregulation, relationship difficulties, and impulsivity. In our clinical trials, treatment begins with an assessment to identify which patterns are driving a particular patient's symptoms, followed by an individualized plan emphasizing the most relevant modules.

BPD Compass takes 18 sessions, considerably shorter than many established BPD treatments. Like GPM, it was designed for generalist clinicians without requiring extensive specialized training.

In an initial randomized clinical trial, participants receiving BPD Compass showed large improvements in BPD symptoms. Subsequent research also documented improvements in emotional, relational, and impulsive functioning, as well as symptoms of co-occurring conditions.

BPD is an incredibly heterogeneous condition. Some people experience frequent suicidal crises and need intensive, long-term support. Others may make meaningful progress with a shorter intervention.

As a BPD treatment developer, my goal isn't to replace existing treatments. It's to expand the options available. Not everyone with BPD needs year-long therapy, and we should have effective, personalized alternatives for those who don't.

Ultimately, the best treatment is one that addresses your difficulties, provides the support you need, and is accessible enough for you to receive it.

If you or someone you love is contemplating suicide, seek help immediately. For help 24/7, dial 988 for the 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline, or reach out to the Crisis Text Line by texting TALK to 741741. To find a therapist near you, visit the Psychology Today Therapy Directory.

Looks like Lawrence Sullivan settled his differences with Rockstar.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Remember in the closing days of 2023, when Rockstar dropped the first trailer for Grand Theft Auto 6? Amidst the everything it had going on was a brief shot of a man with extensive facial tattoos, who was quickly called out by people hip to such things as a knockoff of Florida Joker, aka Lawrence Sullivan, a Florida man who went viral for his 2017 mug shot.

Sullivan himself called it out in a since-deleted TikTok video, first telling Rockstar "we gotta talk" and then, with no response forthcoming, threatening legal action. Not long after that, though, he put forth a new offer: "Let me voice the character, give me more storyline in the game… Let's make history happen, man."

It seems that Sullivan got what he wanted: In a video posted to Instagram, Sullivan said he was given a role in GTA 6.

"I'm an official voice actor on GTA 6, the game." Sullivan says. "For the last three years a lot of people were saying I was not going to be in the game, I was not going to be nothing, they were destroying my mental health. They were saying a lot of fucked shit. But now it's time for me to shit on them and show them what the fuck Florida Joker is about!"

"Y'all gonna see. Special voice actor for GTA 6. This ain't no gimmick, this ain't no lie!"

A post shared by Florida joker 🃏 (@floridaajoker)

I had doubts, but this apparently really ain't no gimmick or lie. Rockstar has posted the full GTA 6 credits and sure enough, Lawrence Sullivan is in there. Now, it could be a different Lawrence Sullivan, sure, but under the circumstances I don't think that's very likely.

Sullivan's role doesn't appear to be a particularly prominent one: He's credited, along with a great many other people, as one of "The Local Population." But at the end of the day, he made it: Florida Joker really is in GTA 6, and he seems pretty chuffed about it. I'd call that a happy ending.

Payoneer Receives OCC Conditional Approval to Form Stablecoin National Trust Bank

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NEW YORK, Nov. 20, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Payoneer, the global financial technology company powering business growth across borders, today announced that it has received preliminary conditional approval from the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to form PAYO Digital Bank, a national trust bank designed to support stablecoin functionality for global business. Today's approval creates a path for Payoneer to build a federally regulated foundation for the future of cross-border commerce, supporting the businesses as they adopt stablecoins and simplifying how they pay and get paid globally. PAYO Digital Bank is now able to operate in its organizational phase, while it seeks final approval to open for business.

Once PAYO Digital Bank receives final OCC approval to open and issue payment stablecoins, it plans to issue PAYO-USD, a fully reserved, GENIUS Act-compliant stablecoin. PAYO-USD, which will not be listed on exchanges, will serve as a balance within the Payoneer ecosystem that will drive interoperability between traditional and blockchain-based currencies. This delivers real-world utility, giving businesses access to safe stablecoin balances and allowing for frictionless movement into local currencies where they operate. This builds on Payoneer's existing network, delivering local currency payouts in 190+ markets.

"The promise of stablecoin is to simplify global commerce. That can only happen when stablecoin is useful in the markets where our customers operate," said Rob Morgan, CEO of PAYO Digital Bank. "Morer than one third of our customers already use stablecoin today. PAYO-USD is designed to improve the experience, making it seamless for businesses to convert their stablecoin balances into the local currencies needed to run their business, giving them more choice, faster movement of funds, and an easier way to transact globally."

PAYO Digital Bank will offer a wide range of stablecoin services, allowing customers to hold stablecoin balances in bank-approved digital wallets, enable businesses to pay or be paid in a variety of third-party stablecoins, and convert their balances into fiat in the local markets where they operate. The bank will operate under direct federal oversight, enabling global businesses to adopt stablecoin payments from a safe and trusted partner.

Payoneer is the financial platform for cross-border business and global payments. Payoneer empowers millions of businesses with the financial tools and services they need to grow and transact globally with confidence. Payoneer makes it easier for businesses, particularly in emerging markets, to connect to the global economy, pay and get paid across borders, manage their funds across multiple currencies, and grow their businesses.

For more information, visit https://www.payoneer.com/

Why The Flying Wing Never Became A Commercial Airliner

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jack Northrop envisioned a sleek, tailless future when he sent the YB-49 aloft on October 21, 1947, stretching a 172-foot (52.4 meters) wing across the desert sky without a conventional fuselage or vertical stabilizer. Promising lower drag and greater efficiency than traditional layouts, the experimental jet bomber nevertheless battled stability challenges before the second prototype crashed on June 5, 1948, killing all five crew members aboard and leading to the program winding down between 1949 and 1950.

Despite the allure of an all-wing silhouette, commercial aviation stuck with cylindrical fuselages and tailed configurations that offered predictable handling, straightforward cabin pressurization, and familiar airport compatibility. Now that digital flight controls and blended-wing concepts are bringing tailless aerodynamics back into the spotlight, what technical, operational, and commercial hurdles still stand between a flying wing concept and a viable passenger airliner?

Converted from two piston-engined YB-35 airframes, the YB-49 stretched to a 172-foot (52.4-meter) wingspan and measured 53 feet, 1 inch (16.2 meters) in length, trading propeller drives for eight Allison J35 turbojets buried inside the wing, according to The Little Aviation Museum. Removing a conventional fuselage and tail assembly allowed almost the entire planform to contribute to lift with reduced parasite drag, helping the prototype reach roughly 493 mph (793 km/h). At the same time, early turbojet fuel burn and internal volume tradeoffs tempered its range and payload flexibility relative to piston-powered designs.

Flying without a tall vertical tail meant the YB-49 had less natural stability in yaw and had delicate pitch and roll-yaw coupling, including dutch roll, during turbulent air, maneuvers, and simulated bombing runs. As noted by MiGFlug, four small vertical fins were fitted to aid directional damping, though in the late 1940s crews relied on mechanical linkages and human coordination rather than high-speed digital flight control computers to manage continuous stability corrections.

After its maiden flight on October 21, 1947, the program flew only two converted airframes before the second prototype crashed on June 5, 1948, claiming the lives of all five crew members on board. Production orders were canceled in 1949 as Air Force leadership shifted toward competing bomber programs, and formal flight testing concluded in March 1950 before the surviving airframes were scrapped.

For an aerodynamically unstable tailless wing to become a dependable aircraft and actually work, it requires replacing purely mechanical linkages with high-speed digital fly-by-wire flight-control computers. Motion sensors can continuously measure aircraft attitude and rate changes, feeding real-time data to flight computers that command rapid, micro-adjusted deflections across trailing-edge control surfaces. That closed-loop automation damps pitch, yaw, and dutch roll oscillations far faster and more consistently than manual pilot inputs alone could achieve in earlier eras.

Digital flight control design made the Northrop B-2 Spirit possible by allowing a pure flying wing to achieve low-observable radar signatures without needing a conventional vertical tail. Instead of using a large fin that would compromise stealth, the B-2 uses computer-coordinated elevons and split drag rudders to generate controlled yawing moments and maintain crisp handling across varying weights and flight regimes. Computing redundancy and sensor voting became foundational to keeping a naturally neutral or unstable wing profile predictable from takeoff through landing.

Decades later, the Northrop B-21 Raider extends that digital control lineage into a modern low-observable bomber program built on newer avionics and computing architectures. The B-2 and B-21 demonstrate that computerized flight controls conquered the core stability and control challenges of tailless military aircraft, but solving stability in a combat airframe did not automatically translate into an airline cabin, and how to make it a reality.

Flying wing bombers nearly entered service during WWII and soon after, however, they would become reality until the B-2 in the 1990s.

The pure flying wing has not necessarily been revived, but JetZero is advancing the Z4 as a blended wing body (BWB) concept designed to carry roughly 230 to 250 passengers by smoothly merging a wide, lifting center body into outer wings. The program has gained traction through a $235 million US Air Force award supporting full-scale demonstrator development alongside plans to establish manufacturing operations in North Carolina. With a full-scale demonstrator targeted to fly in late 2027, the roadmap aims for commercial entry into service in the early 2030s if technical and regulatory hurdles are cleared on schedule.

Commercial interest in the Z4 is very real, and carriers such as Japan Airlines and United Airlines have made agreements to support the development of the aircraft. For airlines evaluating next-generation single-aisle and midsize replacements, a BWB promises lower drag and reduced fuel burn per seat compared with conventional tube-and-wing layouts. Making those conditional fleet commitments become firm deliveries, however, depends on proving that the airframe can meet strict safety, manufacturing, and operational standards at scale.

Propulsion integration sits near the center of that verification effort, including a November 2026 report from Leeham News noting that the FAA is funding a study into top-mounted engine airflow for JetZero's design. Placing engines above the aft airframe can help shield noise, but maintaining clean airflow and stable engine operation across flight conditions remains a critical engineering focus ahead of commercial certification.

A cylindrical tube naturally resists internal cabin pressure by distributing hoop stress evenly around a circular cross-section, whereas a wide, flattened wing-body hull faces far higher bending and pressure-loading demands. A non-cylindrical cabin shell tends to bow outward when pressurized at cruise altitude, so to compensate, designers need to add structural reinforcement, internal bulkheads, or load-bearing frames that add mass and complexity.

Emergency evacuation rules further add to cabin design complexity, particularly the regulatory requirement to evacuate a full aircraft within 90 seconds using half of the available exits, covered in Simple Flying analysis. Unlike a narrow single- or twin-aisle tube where passengers move linearly toward well-marked doors, a wide blended-wing cabin needs carefully engineered pathways, clear signage, and balanced exit placement, so travelers seated deep inside the layout can reach exits without bottlenecks. Cabin tiering across premium and economy zones also needs to align with aisle geometry and exit capacity rather than treating interior floor space as an open hall.

On the ground, widebody wingspans around 213 feet (65 meters) must fit within existing terminal gate envelopes, taxiway clearances, and apron stand markings without forcing costly airport rebuilds. Even when a wide airframe clears wingspan limits at a standard widebody gate, its broader center section and altered fuselage contour can complicate jetbridge docking reach, de-icing access, and ground support vehicle positioning. Smooth ramp operations really depend on proving that turnaround tasks load and unload cleanly alongside conventional airliners.

Several companies, including Airbus, JetZero, and Boeing, are actively developing prototypes.

Within a wide, open cabin, the sensory rhythm of a flight changes compared with walking down a narrow, low-ceilinged aisle. Designers can shape the atmosphere through layered ambient lighting, tactile fabrics, and warmer material palettes that soften the industrial feel of long-haul travel and create distinct zones for rest, work, or socializing. When the interior feels less like a corridor and more like a curated living space, passenger stress often eases.

Sightlines and motion perception play a major role in whether that spaciousness feels inviting or disorienting. Travelers seated further outboard from the centerline can notice more pronounced vertical movement when the aircraft banks or hits chop, making smooth cabin damping and calm visual anchors especially valuable. At the same time, passengers placed in central rows away from outer walls miss natural views of the horizon, requiring thoughtful lighting cues or digital view panels to keep the cabin feeling open and grounded.

For airlines, an unconventional cabin layout offers a rare canvas for brand differentiation in a crowded market. Carriers can build an identity around quiet acoustics, signature color schemes, and tailored service rituals that signal innovation without feeling gimmicky. If passengers connect emotionally with the warmth and poise of the space, the cabin itself becomes part of the airline's promise, not something that needs to be explained to customers.

Pure flying wings remained absent from passenger service because winning aerodynamic efficiency on paper is only half the battle when weighed against structural pressure loads, emergency evacuation rules, airport compatibility, and airline economics. Digital flight controls solved the stability hurdles that haunted the YB-49 and paved the way for stealth bombers, but turning a wide, wing-shaped cabin into a profitable, certified airliner is no easy task.

Any manufacturer brave enough to try and make something like this has to solve practical constraints that circular fuselages and conventional tails handle with far less friction. Whether a blended wing body can actually fill that divide comes down to proving real-world reliability, passenger acceptance, and unit economics without turning every airport gate or cabin layout into a costly exception.

The next major checkpoints arrive with full-scale demonstrator flight testing targeted for late 2027 and potential commercial entry into service in the early 2030s. If JetZero or other ventures like Nautilus can validate aerodynamic performance, propulsion integration, and regulatory certification along that timeline, blended-wing designs could finally move from aviation curiosity to active airline fleets.

I Thought Rocky And Creed's Big Fight Scene Would Be The End Of I Play Rocky. So What Happened?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

The Reactions To Brad Pitt’s New Cliff Booth Movie Are Wild, And I Didn’t Expect One Reference

The Cliff Booth Reviews Point Out A Missed Opportunity In Brad Pitt’s Sequel, And I Have To Agree

I Don't Think We're Going To Get A Street Fighter II, And Here's Why I'm Okay With That

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Spoilers for I Play Rocky are ahead! You can catch the movie in theaters now.

When I think about the best sports movies, often the big match or game at the center of it comes to mind immediately. For instance, with Rocky, the titular character’s fight with Apollo Creed is something I instantly picture when the film gets brought up. So, I was surprised to see the fight wasn’t really featured within the story of I Play Rocky, the project on the 2026 movie schedule about the making of Rocky. Instead, it was used during the credits.

It turns out there is a reason why that big match was cut from the film and moved into the credits.

Now, even though they took a “week” to film the fight between Rocky and Creed for this movie, it was eventually cut…kind of. Ultimately, the scene was moved to the credits. So, why did that happen? Well, director Peter Farrelly told me the match was actually supposed to be the end of I Play Rocky. However, a big realization about the stairs scene changed his mind, as he recalled during an interview with CinemaBlend:

And by the way, it wasn't originally supposed to be over the end credits. It was supposed to be the end of the movie. But when you get in an editing room, as good as the script is, you have to let the movie find its way. And when he goes up the top of those stairs [and raises his arms up], there was such a high watching it that you couldn't get there again.

While the director was very open about this being the right call with me, he also admitted that it took him a while to fully accept that. It took a long time to film the fight, and it’s such an iconic moment from Rocky. However, he eventually came to the conclusion that they had to move it to the credits:

Like even though we took a lot of time making that fight and doing it, you will never get that high again. You're not going to feel it. And so I showed it to a few people in the editing room. They go, ‘You should end on the top of the stairs.’ I was like, ‘No, I took a week. I took a week to shoot the fight. I'm not cutting the fight.’ And it took me a couple weeks to finally realize I have to cut the fight. It's got to end on the stairs.

I know I was a bit shocked not to see the fight prominently featured in the movie, when so many of the other scenes are. However, I did think it was a cool surprise to cut it into the credits, and it served as a full-circle moment to the scene where we watch Anthony Ippolito’s Sylvester Stallone and Stephan James’ Carl Weathers train for the fight.

Speaking of Ippolito and James, they, like Stallone and Weathers, trained and learned how to box for this movie. That means they took a few punches too. When I asked the Sly actor about filming the fight between Rocky and Creed, he said:

We took some real we took some real shots during that. Me and Stephan were just trying to figure it out, and you know, sometimes we messed up here and there, and we tag each other, but it was all right.

James also confirmed that they took some hits while preparing for and filming this movie. He revealed that he and Ippolito trained together as well, and “got close in a very short period of time,” noting that they were “in the trenches with each other.” That became very clear too, as he told me:

I may have taken a punch or two that I wasn't looking forward to. But you know, had to do what I had to do to pull it off. And I think it was all worth it that we were able to, you know, not only step into these guys psychologically, but physically as well.

Well, considering the amount of time, effort and punches that went into this, I’m happy that Rocky and Creed’s fight did find its way into the movie…even if it wasn’t in the way it was originally intended.

Now, if you’d like to see how this legendary sequence is shown in the credits of the well-reviewed I Play Rocky, you can catch the film in theaters now. And if you’d like to compare their take on the sequence to Rocky’s, you can stream the OG movie with a Paramount+ subscription.

She graduated with her master’s degree in arts journalism and communications from the Newhouse School at Syracuse University. Since joining the CB team she has covered numerous TV shows and movies -- including her personal favorite shows Ted Lasso and The Marvelous Mrs. Maisel. She also has followed and consistently written about everything from Taylor Swift to Fire Country, and she's enjoyed every second of it.

Barnsley FC 1 Sheffield Wednesday 2: Headstart costs Reds against clinical Owls

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On a night dedicated to Weston Park's cancer work, the Reds extended that charity to their defending, and within 15 minutes, Wednesday already looked uncatchable.

Vimal Yoganathan's sixth goal in eight games ensured a proper South Yorkshire derby broke out, but the Reds are getting rather too good at nearly or semi-performances for their own comfort down in the League One relegation zone.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

After Yoganathan's strike, the home side showed the fight they should be a given in games against the near-neighbours, but it was already too late.

For the visitors, it was a character test passed in front of 5,700 invigilators in the away end, Jamal Lowe scoring from their only two shots on target on a night that felt like a big step forward for their new manager, Dave Challinor.

"Three points away ole, ole," sang the packed away end and more pertinently, "Wednesday are back."

Funny how one week can totally change the mood of a football club.

The Reds actually started the better, but they are getting good at looking good in ineffectual parts of the field, and awful at the business ends.

Their fans had been in mutinous mood before the game, hundreds gathering outside the ground before kick-off to protest about the way things are being run, but there was no such defiance when they needed it early on.

Lowe – playing more of an orthodox winger role with the balance of Wednesday's unchanged side titled more towards a back four than a three than it had been the previous week – was ruthless in exploiting it.

When Barry Bannan lofted a free-kick to the back post in the ninth minute, Liam Cooper was in far too much space there. The defender headed the ball across goal, and Lowe pounced for the Owls' third set-piece goal in a week.

Five minutes later, two passes took the visitors from the halfway line to a second goal.

Callum Slattery's pass to Bannan was almost cut out but when he found Lowe, but Jubril Okedina stood off, almost daring the forward to score, so he did.

Barnsley's early work lacked that conviction.

Reyes Cleary's second-minute snapshot lacked the power to beat Joe Lumley, and Ryan Brownlie's also allowed the goalkeeper to save. Makenzie Kirk miskicked a shot when Cleary picked him out, and when Patrick Kelly ran onto a knockdown by the centre-forward, his shot lacked power.

Cleary put a shot well wide and Luca Connell hammered one into Billy Mitchell.

Ziyad Larkeche controlled a fairly routine Cieran Slicker pass over the touchline.

But a side with seven starters under the age of 23 plugged away and got back into it courtesy of one of their young leaders.

Like Okedina, Yan Valery stood off Cleary and when the winger delivered a low cross, Yoganathan arrived onto it like a centre-forward to pull a goal back.

With both sides attacking their own noisy ends, it made for an entertaining second half.

Like Barnsley at the start of the first half, Wednesday pushed the harder, without making it count.

Bannan had a shot blocked and when Slicker came out of his goal to stop former Red Davis Keillor-Dunn finishing a Bannan pass, Lowe lifted his shot onto the roof of the net.

At the other end, Barnsley centre-back Sam Gale forced his way to the byline, but his cross cleared those in the area. Larkeche put an acrobatic effort wide.

It was Wednesday who were throwing on the substitutes - a sign of their deeper resources, but also off their keenness to finally add to Lowe's second shot on target, 14 minutes in.

Despite one of those substitutes, Harry Gray, accelerating past Gale for a chance Slattery had blocked, it never came, but it did not need to because hard as Barnsley pushed – throwing centre-back Connor O'Riordan on as a late emergency striker and sending Slicker up for a stoppage-time corner – they could not find the next goal either.

Luca Connell came the closest, his free-kick tipped over in the 93rd minute.

His team are just not good enough to gift promotion wannabes like Wednesday a two-goal headstart and get away with it. Only just, you might say, but game after game that is enough.

Barnsley: Slicker; Okedina (Reid 86), Gale, Kay, Larkeche; Connell, Kelly (Johnson 86); Brownlie (O'Riordan 90), Yoganathan (Noslin 81), Cleary; Kirk (Toure 81).

Sheffield Wednesday: Lumley; Valery, Bernard (Santos 86), Cooper, Swinkels (Palmer 70); Heskey, Mitchell (Barry 70), Slattery, Bannan (Gray 78), J Lowe (Liongola 70); Keillor-Dunn.

Roger Craig Smith - The Big Phantom Blade Zero Interview

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

Chances are, if you’ve played a video game, you’ve heard the voice of Roger Craig Smith. He’s the video game voice of Sonic the Hedgehog, plays Chris Redfield in the Resident Evil series, and portrays Ezio Auditore da Firenze in Assassin’s Creed.

His 20-year career has seen him appear in almost 200 video games, playing original characters, dubbing international ones, and voicing many of the medium’s greatest stories.

His latest role comes as the English dub of Phantom Blade Zero’s protagonist, Soul. Following the overnight success of the Game of the Year contender, we sat down with Craig Smith to discuss it, including how it came to be last minute, as well as, of course, some chat about Sonic.

Jia Xiongkai is the voice of Phantom Blade Zero’s Soul in its native Chinese. For the longest time, Xiongkai’s English counterpart was Jay Makensuk; however, at the last minute, Craig Smith stepped into the character’s shoes. I had to find out why.

“It was, I guess, late into the game. But that's also fairly common for what this effectively was, which was a localization,” he tells me. “The game would have essentially been done before this happened. It's funny, I guess it's maybe a little unorthodox for a video game, but not so much for a dubbing project.”

How much did he know about replacing Makensuk, though?

“The elephant in the room is the fact that the early dev examples released featured another actor. I don't know the circumstances, but obviously someone else was in the role before that,” he says. “I honestly don’t know why it was, this stuff just happens in the business. I’ve had it go against me in the same way. You’re doing something, and all of a sudden, somebody decides they want to change course.”

“I’ve done vertical slices where I’ve thought that I was going to be the final choice, and it was like ‘maybe not, maybe this was just to help obtain the financing they wanted for the game’. I’ve had things taken away from me at the last minute; I’ve had things fall into my lap, but I never celebrate until the check clears the bank and the project is released.”

Despite playing the English version of Soul, Craig Smith feels it’s equally as important, if not more, to play through the game in its native language, even if that means not hearing his voice.

“If you want to understand what the tone was intended to be and to understand and even broaden your horizons culturally, to kind of hear cadences and delivery styles and the way that protagonists are approached in different cultures, you would want to experience it in that way first,” he says. “I say that as somebody who's been lucky enough to be hired to be the English dubbing voice for tons of games.”

Phantom Blade Zero is rooted in Chinese culture and folklore; does this make voicing a character like Soul more difficult?

“It’s really like threading a needle,” he tells me. “You need to understand the source material and the performance style while trying to blend in more of an English-speaking style of dialogue. You have to try and make sure it works a little bit better for the English-speaking ear.”

“It’s a fascinating part of what we do, trying to make sure all markets are covered, and that people get to experience things in the best way possible for them”

Dubbing is a big part of what Craig Smith does. He’s the English voice of Daigo Dojima in the Yakuza series, and has appeared in Naruto, Bleach, and Aggretsuko. How different is dubbing a character from being the original voice?

“It’s very different. You’re not creating the character, it’s more of an homage,” he says. “You're trying to make sure that you're not stepping on the toes of anything. I don't know that you necessarily get to make it your own. You're trying to give reverence to an original performer and yet also do something in a style that makes sense for maybe localizing”.

I love to have a deeper understanding, especially at this phase of my career. I want to give my all for the audience.

Craig Smith mentioned earlier that projects often come about last minute. It reminded me of a conversation I had 18 months ago with Alix Wilton Regan, Aliona Baranova, Rasmus Hardiker, and Pepe Balderrama about Lies of P. Two of the actors preferred to get their scripts in advance to prepare their lines, while the other two preferred to dive straight into them in the booth—which did Craig Smith prefer, the ones with more time to prepare or the last-minute projects?

“Both have their pluses and minuses, I suppose. There can be an energetic element to the latter approach of just jumping in and going for it. And certain projects really lend themselves to that,” he says. “Now that I'm 20-plus years in the business, I very often ask for stuff in advance. I love to get stuff because I want to come in with some choices. I want to understand some things. It depends on the project. It depends on the character. But I love to have a deeper understanding, especially at this phase of my career. I want to give my all for the audience.”

With Craig Smith’s role in Phantom Blade Zero very much falling into the “dive straight into the booth” camp, was there anything he saw that made him think that the game would be as much of a runaway success that it is?

“I couldn’t believe it. It surprises me that anything gets an audience these days with every device vying for our time, so it’s amazing, but with this game, it wasn’t necessarily a surprise,” he tells me. “It’s right in line with how much work the development team put in. It’s aesthetically beautiful, the fight mechanics are incredible, and there is so much to chew on with the story. Maybe I’m a little surprised, but I’m just so thrilled, I really am.”

Perhaps Craig Smith’s most notable role is the video game version of Sonic the Hedgehog. He’s played the character for 16 years, first appearing in Sonic Colors and Sonic Free Riders back in 2010. For many, he is the Blue Blur, at least until Ben Schwartz stepped into his big red shoes for the live-action silver-screen adaptation.

Smith has always been super complimentary about Schwartz’s version of the character, but he’s never explicitly said whether he was sad that he didn’t get the role, so I had to ask.

“What do you think? I mean, what do you think? I mean, like, no, I don't feel like being a part of some of the biggest movies on Earth,” he says chuckling. “It’s like asking me if I’d come back as Ezio in Assassin’s Creed. What do you think?”

“I mean, as actors we're inherently envious and jealous and, you know, we want to do it all. We want so bad for you to like us, you know. So, of course, I would have. That's always been my biggest hurt, that I never even got a chance, that it was not even an option to get in and have a meeting or anything. It just never happened. The industry's weird, but I get it. It is what it is. But yes, of course, I would have killed for a chance to get in there. I get I might not be right for you, but having the opportunity is the first and foremost thing for me.”

He couldn’t talk about the Sonic Movie version of the character without heaping praise on Schwartz, though.

“I've stated so many times, I'm like, you couldn't ask for a more wonderful human being and a more talented, creative genius in Ben Schwartz to be in the role, doing such a great job with it and instantly understanding the fan base. There’s so much attention on this franchise again, and a big part of that is the success of the films. It’s really reinvigorated the entire brand.”

There is nothing but good, good stuff. I look back fondly at every role.

With my time with Craig Smith running short, I wanted to ask more about his illustrious career. He’s touched many of gaming’s biggest franchises, but there are still a lot that remain free of his voice. Is there any he’s desperate to appear in, or sad that he hasn’t?

“No, not really. And what an unbelievably fortunate position to be in to say that. I look at it like lightning has struck many times in my life. And I'm very, very lucky,” he says. “Maybe deep down there's some stuff in there. But the reality is, I would not be doing myself any service by dwelling on that. And instead just pinching myself repeatedly and going, ‘can you believe that you woke up on your 30th birthday with essentially no career?’”

“I've reached a certain phase and an age where I just go. I cannot believe this. There is nothing but good, good stuff. I look back fondly at every role, and my attitude always remains the same: I just want to make everybody on the other side of the glass happy.”

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

This Egg Wash Secret Makes the Prettiest Pie Crusts Every Time

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The secret to a beautifully golden Thanksgiving pie might be the simplest ingredient in your kitchen.

Thanksgiving pies have a way of bringing out the perfectionist in even the most relaxed bakers. Yet you might spend half an hour creating the perfect pie dough design, carefully crimping the edges or cutting little leaves from scraps of pastry, only to pull the finished pie from the oven and discover that your beautiful handiwork has browned unevenly. It’s a small disappointment in the grand scheme of things, but after all that effort, a little indignation is understandable.

An egg wash is supposed to give pastry a lovely golden color and delicate sheen, but getting it to cooperate isn’t always as simple as beating an egg and picking up a pastry brush. Fortunately, there’s an easy trick that makes a noticeable difference, and it comes courtesy of a familiar face in the baking world: Great British Baking Show judge Paul Hollywood.

Paul’s secret is simply to add a pinch of salt to the beaten egg. He lets it sit for about five minutes before brushing it onto pastry dough. In a BBC Food demonstration, Paul uses the technique to glaze scones, but it works just as well for giving pie crusts a beautiful, even finish.

Beat an egg with a small pinch of salt until they’re well combined, then set the mixture aside for a few minutes. Give it another quick stir before brushing a thin, even layer over the pastry. The amount of salt won’t affect the flavor, and there’s no reason to make any other adjustments to your favorite pie recipe.

The difference is subtle but useful. Instead of clinging stubbornly to your pastry brush or collecting in the little crevices of a crimped edge, the egg wash spreads more easily.

If you’ve ever tried brushing a plain beaten egg onto pastry, you’ve probably noticed that it can be surprisingly thick and stringy. That’s because the proteins in the egg give it structure—a nuisance if you need to spread it evenly. Adding salt loosens those proteins, making the egg wash thinner and more fluid after a few minutes. It glides over the pastry more smoothly, leaving a light, even coating rather than stringy puddles.

The egg itself is what gives pastry its golden color and glossy sheen. Salt doesn’t make the crust magically browner; it simply helps the egg wash do its job better. The result is a more consistent finish, particularly around the edges and decorative details where egg wash tends to collect.

A pinch of salt won’t turn a lopsided pie into a masterpiece, but it can help a well-made crust look its best. And after we’ve spent an unreasonable amount of time weaving strips of pastry into a lattice for a Thanksgiving pie, I think we’re entitled to a little help with the finishing touches.

Who is Johannes Zuch supposed to be in GTA 6?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"Every tale begins with an unwritten page quietly awaiting the ink of destiny..."

Everyone is a target in Grand Theft Auto 6. Even Jonathan Blow, the puzzle game designer known for The Witness and Order of the Sinking Star, isn’t safe.

Like previous games in the series, GTA 6 features a handful of playable games within the game. (In an interview with Polygon, Rockstar North co-head Rob Nelson said that the studio wanted to put some previous games in GTA 6, but the tech made that too challenging.) One of those games is Tenebrae Lux, an arthouse game filled with obtuse puzzles and pretentious monologues. It feels like a parody of The Witness right away, and that’s all but confirmed when the opening credits list its auteur developer as Johannes Zuch. Johannes is close to Jonathan, and Zuch sure sounds like “suck,” doesn’t it? Rockstar even lists Zuch as the sole creator in GTA 6’s final credits, despite the fact that it was made by a small team within the studio.

It’s a hysterical in-joke for those who are familiar with Blow, but it’s also a shining example of why Rockstar Games is so good at what it does. It’s a studio that fully commits to its bits, even when it means creating a whole game to do so.

You’ll first come across Tenebrae Lux early in GTA 6’s story. During a mission in Chapter 1, Cal comes over to Jason’s house to help him redecorate for Lucia’s arrival following her unexpected release from prison. After setting up the TV, Cal insists that the two take a break and play some games on the JoySystem. (Side note: If you’re playing on Xbox, there’s certainly some cognitive dissonance seeing a clear PS5 reference on screen.) He fires up Tenebrae Lux, hyping it up as a real game by a visionary developer. “He would love to work with other people, but he’s just too fucking good at everything,” Cal says of Johannes Zuch, whose name appears in every single opening credit as the game begins, including some comically ostentatious ones that are just outside the realm of believability, like “original idea” and “mise en scène.”

The game itself is a black-and-white first-person puzzle game where you play as a man (voiced by Zuch, of course) who washed up on an island. As you walk around the starting area, listening to Zuch wax poetic in a highfalutin voice, you discover some scattered board game puzzles that require you to move pieces around to wipe other pieces off the board. It’s a pretty direct hit on The Witness’ brand of puzzles, where you have to learn the rules of a format without any tutorials. And it’s also a direct hit on Jonathan Blow’s brand of ostentatious writing; Tenebrae Lux sounds so much like Order of the Sinking Star (which released one month ago) that you’ll think that Blow must have had a Rockstar mole working within his studio, Thekla Inc., during development.

Tenebrae Lux only gets more obnoxious (complimentary) from there when you return to it later by playing it in Jason and Lucia’s home. The next major puzzle throws you into a series of looping, nearly identical rooms. You can only escape through a big-brained puzzle that is so unfathomably obtuse that even a thoroughly written guide would sound like gibberish. It’s completely maddening, as Zuch won’t stop prattling on about the Sisyphean hell he’s trapped in while an old-timey sea shanty plays over and over on a crackling record player.

As the kind of person who earnestly loves this kind of game, I will be the first to admit it: They got my ass real good. Tenebrae Lux is a pitch-perfect parody of a very specific kind of elevated art game; it’s as much a light dunk on the philosophical wandering of The Talos Principle as it is The Witness. Whether it's in good taste for a company as big as Rockstar to dunk on that kind of game is up to debate, but what's impressive is that it’s not a throwaway joke that’s on-screen for 60 seconds. Rockstar sells the gag by turning it into a full game that you can play at any time.

That level of commitment to an out-there idea is everywhere in GTA 6. Quick visual gags turn into entire questlines. Your phone’s news app isn’t just full of goofy headlines, but fully written articles that are hysterical. Magazines are populated with feature articles that expertly ape the tone of your average grocery store glossy. Even your Instagram-like Snapmatic feed is filled with authentic shitposts and even more authentic replies. All of this gives Leonida feel like a real place with a very distinct sense of personality. If you don’t have the time to play through the entirety of GTA 6, you can understand the appeal of Rockstar’s all-in attitude through Tenebrae Lux alone.

And if it sounds like GTA 6 is punching down on indie games, don’t worry: Rockstar takes potshots at itself and other AAA studios in the same breath. When Cal fires up Tenebrae Lux during the mission, he drops a killer meta joke as it loads. “You can see it on the screen man, it’s so fucking raw,” Cal tells Jason in a scene that very closely resembles the famous sequence from 2016’s Uncharted 4 where Nathan Drake and Elena Fisher play a round of Crash Bandicoot together. “None of just sitting around waiting for people to finish talking. Come on, are we watching a movie or playing a game here!?”

“Cool, can we play?” a bored Jason finally says, cutting the chat short. I’m sure some players will feel the same after patching their roof and fixing their stairs in GTA 6’s early missions.

The follow up to The Witness is dense, meticulously designed, and full of contradictions

10 New Movies to Watch This Weekend on Netflix, Prime Video, and HBO Max (November 20 - 22)

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The biggest movie of the year, Netflix's latest animated movie, and more

Happy weekend! The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping is out in theaters, seemingly putting an end to one of the biggest franchises in recent years. It’s pretty good.

If you prefer to stay home, you have plenty of options. Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey is out on VOD and finally available to watch on your TV. The movie stars Matt Damon as Odysseus, following him and his crew of men as they try to return home in the aftermath of the devastating Trojan War.

Other major releases include Steps, Netflix’s latest animated movie, following Cinderella’s evil stepsisters, with one of them forced to team up with her in order to save their realm. It’s voiced by Amanda Seyfried, Ali Wong, Stephanie Tsu, Daniel Radcliffe, and more.

If you’re looking for horror or drama with a healthy dose of disturbing material, Netflix has two options: Insidious: Out of the Further follows a mom plagued by awful nightmares that might be missives from a parallel dimension; Gentle Monster follows a pianist and a mother who moves to the country with her family looking for a break from city life. Then her husband is accused by the police of serious crimes.

Other horror movies are available on VOD. There’s The Ice Cream Man, following an ice cream seller whose frozen desserts turn kids into killers, and Appofeniacs, a series of horror snippets exploring AI.

Other highlights include Nicolas Cage's Best Pancakes in the County, the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony, and the Patricia Highsmith-inspired psychological thriller A Talent for Murder. There’s also The Beast, a new Samuel L. Jackson film where he stars as a President trying to survive a terrorist attack with only a wounded Secret Service Agent for protection. Happy watching.

Perhaps the biggest film of the year is finally available on streaming. Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey adapts the story of Odysseus (Matt Damon) to the screen in appropriately epic fashion and format, having him face giants, witches, sea monsters, mermaids, and much more as he tries to return home to his wife (Anne Hathaway) and son (Tom Holland).

The Odyssey opens with a gentle warning: "A time of apparent magic," a gentle prod at audiences to both accept and question what they're about to see. Yet Ithaca, and the Palace of Odysseus, is all too devoid of magic. His wife Penelope (Anne Hathaway) has spent the last 20 years waiting for her husband (Matt Damon) to return home from the Trojan War, while a small army of suitors vies to replace him. Running out of time before lead suitor Antinous (Robert Pattinson) can seal the deal, Odysseus' son Telemachus (Tom Holland) sets out on a journey to either find his dad or prove he's dead and claim the crown (either option seems fine for the young lad).

Netflix’s latest animated film takes the well-trodden story of Cinderella and reinvents it through a fresh perspective. It tells the story from the side of her evil stepsisters. The story follows Lilith (Ali Wong), who accidentally turns her sister Margot (Stephanie Tsu) into a frog. To bring her sister back, she has to team up with her estranged stepsister Cinderella (Amanda Seyfried) to save the kingdom.

The star-studded cast of Steps works hard to give depth to characters who, for all intents and purposes, were completely shallow in their original tales. Wong is known for her sharpness and her comedy. She meshes well with this more modern take on the ugly stepsister, but she’s just as capable of hitting you where it hurts when the story calls for it. Her anger at being unaccepted and belittled by the people around her, while Cinderella only has to exist to be loved, is heartbreaking to watch.

Insidious: Out of the Further is finally out on Netflix. The film expands on the successful Blumhouse franchise, focusing on a different family. It follows Gemma (Amelia Eve), a single mother traumatized by a childhood dominated by an absent mother and a terrifying incident. As she begins to have strange dreams, she starts to scare her daughter and mirror her mother’s erratic behavior.

Written and directed by Mary Kreutzer, Gentle Monster follows a concert pianist (Lea Seydoux) who relocates to the countryside after her husband claims burnout. As the film progresses, we learn that her husband is facing some serious accusations from the police.

Based on a pivotal summer in Anthony Bourdain’s life, Tony is set in 1975, following a 19-year-old fictional version of Bourdain who relocates to Provincetown to follow his high school crush. After a series of slips and disasters, he ends up getting a job as a dishwasher in a restaurant.

Nicolas Cage stars in this thriller, which has drawn comparisons to Tarantino, playing a criminal on the run from a botched heist. He hides in a diner, triggering a face-off with all sorts of enemies, including federal agents, criminals, and more, putting a seemingly innocent waitress and line cook at risk.

Eli Roth’s latest film follows a strange and mysterious ice cream man (Ari Millen) who serves frozen desserts that turn kids into violent killers.

Set in 1995, A Talent for Murder follows an editor from New York (Alden Ehrenreich) who’s been sent to the Swiss Alps to convince Patricia Highsmith (Helen Mirren) to write a new Tom Ripley novel. There, she challenges him to help her craft the perfect murder.

Appofeniacs contains various stories, all centered on the impact of AI. It follows a troll wreaking chaos on people’s lives by creating AI deepfakes, including a smear campaign, putting a man's life and career at risk, and more.

The Beast is an action-thriller starring Samuel L. Jackson. The premise is wild: the US President (Jackson), a former U.S. veteran, gets stranded at an international summit, with his protective detail wiped out in an attack. He’s left alone with a wounded Secret Service agent (Joel Kinnaman) as the two try to survive.

Hello Sunshine’s Lauren Neustadter Stepping Down at Year’s End (Exclusive)

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The longtime president of film and television is leaving after nearly a decade at Reese Witherspoon’s company and on the heels of a recent ownership change.

Changes continue at Reese Witherspoon’s Hello Sunshine, where the company’s top creative executive, Lauren Neustadter, is stepping down at year’s end. The longtime collaborators announced the move on Friday afternoon.

“Lauren’s taste, passion and leadership have been integral to Hello Sunshine’s film and television success,” said Witherspoon, founder of Hello Sunshine. “I’m so proud of what we accomplished together and grateful for her partnership and the lasting foundation she built around telling powerful, complex female-driven stories that resonate.” Related Stories Business Reese Witherspoon's Hello Sunshine Breaks Away From Candle Media Business From 'F1' to 'Slow Horses' to 'Call My Agent!': Mediawan Executives Take Madrid Spotlight

“Building Hello Sunshine alongside Reese and our incredible team has been the privilege of a lifetime,” added Neustadter. “I’m deeply proud of all the shows and movies we’ve made, the doors we’ve opened for new voices, and the ways we have put women in the spotlight both in front of and behind the camera. I am grateful for every moment of it, and I will always be cheering for Hello Sunshine.”

Neustadter has served as president of film and television at Hello Sunshine since she joined the company in 2017. Over her tenure, she executive produced a number of TV series, including The Morning Show, Big Little Lies, Little Fires Everywhere, The Last Thing He Told Me, Truth Be Told and Surface — also spearheading adaptations of Reese’s Book Club picks Tiny Beautiful Things and Daisy Jones & The Six. This past summer, Hello Sunshine premiered Legally Blonde prequel Elle at Prime Video and Lucky on Apple TV. Neustadter also worked on features Where the Crawdads Sing, Your Place or Mine, You’re Cordially Invited and TriStar’s upcoming adaptation of The Nightingale. She will remain with the company through the end of the year to support the transition.

Her departure comes during a period of transition for Hello Sunshine. In October, Witherspoon teamed up with Mediawan to buy the company back from Candle Media. Hello Sunshine, which Witherspoon founded with Seth Rodsky in 2016, was acquired by the Blackstone-backed company in 2021 in a deal valued at $900 million.

In partnering with the growing French media company, Hello Sunshine joins a sprawling portfolio of entertainment brands that includes Chernin Entertainment and Plan B Entertainment. But the shift has also meant changes at the company. Hello Sunshine’s sale was followed by layoffs its direct-to-consumer division.

Prior to her near-decade at Hello Sunshine, Neustadter worked at old Fox — where she was senior vice president of programming at the broadcast network, overseeing such series as The Mindy Project and Brooklyn Nine-Nine. She joined the network after a tenure at 20th Century Fox, where she worked on such films as Parental Guidance and Witherspoon vehicle This Means War. While at 20th, she was included The Hollywood Reporter’s Next Gen list — the magazine’s annual assessment of the 35 fastest rising entertainment executives under the age of 35.
