‘War’ Creator George Kay on Writing the Celebrity Divorce Drama, That Shocking Season Finale and the Real Couple Who Inspired a Key Scene: ‘It’s Probably an Awful Moment in Someone’s Life’

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SPOILER ALERT: This article contains major spoilers for “War,” which had its season finale on Nov. 19 on HBO and HBO Max in the U.S., and drops on Nov. 20 on Sky and NOW in the U.K.

Over eight episodes of “War,” actress Carla Duval (Sienna Miller) and tech bro Morgan Henderson (Dominic West) have been squabbling over the couple’s multibillion-dollar fortune as they prepare to part ways for good. The winners, of course, have been the lawyers.

The series centers around two law firms: the modern and fast-paced Taylor & Byrne, founded by Nicholas Taylor (James McArdle) and Serena Byrne (Phoebe Fox), and the stuffy, old-school Cathcarts & Sons, led by St. John Smallwood (Pip Torrens) and Beatrice Ubosi (Nina Sosanya).

If there’s bad blood between Duval and Henderson, relations between the lawyers are worse and bloodier still. Which is perhaps why, in a season finale twist, the divorcing couple decide to negotiate their own settlement amicably, and away from their scheming lawyers.

With Season 1 wrapped up, creator and showrunner George Kay sat down with Variety to talk about some of the real-life inspirations for the show, why he decided on that surprising ending and what we can expect from Season 2, which will see Taylor & Byrne and Cathcarts return with a new case and new clients.

A little bit. Twenty years ago, I had a company that made a documentary series about divorce, and it was quite grim viewing, unsurprisingly. Especially because it was real people, and there was no charisma going on — [“War” director] Ben Taylor wasn’t directing it, basically, and it wasn’t Sienna and Dominic. So I remember having that at the back of my mind, thinking the thing we don’t want here is to make this grim or bleak or anything. And in fact, one of the phrases that we came up with in the writers’ room was that this divorce would be the world’s most expensive couples’ therapy. Even though you don’t physically stay married, you end up closer together.

You want to leave people feeling: “That was good. I liked hanging out with those people.” They were awful a lot of the time, but everyone’s redeemable. And Carla and Morgan don’t despise each other. They just drifted and drifted and drifted.

I wanted to end that story in Season 1, because I wanted him to find his home. And so that was always intended as a season-long story, and really wrapped around that is that thing of “Is it possible to be a good lawyer and a good person?” And I firmly think it is.

We’re not going to extend the espionage thing. But with Johnny, it’s a question of can you ever trust someone who’s done that? Which I think is a relatable thing, whether that’s your relationship or your workplace. If someone’s done something really untrustworthy, can you ever trust them again? So that will linger, and become a big part of Johnny’s story. But the other thing is, we wanted to find a similar character who’s not at the upper echelons of a law firm to really get behind in Season 2. That is Emma Laird, who plays Molly [a junior lawyer at Taylor & Byrne]. We cast Emma, and I remember saying to her, “You’re amazing. Just so you know, please come and do Molly in Season 1, because there’s loads more coming in Season 2.”

Every season, I think it’s worth having someone slightly more junior through which to see the whole thing through a prism of them.

We don’t see her in Season 2. We have guest stars for each season. But we do have a world. So people [in “War”] don’t use X; they use Zephyr. The whole thing keeps kind of bleeding into itself.

That was written with a writer called Toby Finlay, who’s brilliant, and we talked about that a lot in the writers’ room. If you can’t arbitrate on a divorce, you’ll go to litigation, and that’s when it will go public, because it’s not protected anymore. And what’s fun about that is when you’ve got a kind of Morgan Henderson and a Carla Duval, and they are very much in the public eye, that scene triggers the going public of the whole thing. So we were always thinking we want this whole thing to blow up in public on chat shows around “The Milliner,” which is Sienna’s film.

We wanted to put the camera behind the chat show host and the guest, because it’s only when you go on stage, you suddenly get the on-stage eye-view of what it’s like to be on a chat show. We’re so familiar with Graham Norton or Fallon or whatever, seeing it from the front. We felt it was such a private thing to see it from the other side, and then you can hear the lights kind of cracking in the heat, you see the light obfuscating the audience and the pressure of that. She’s such a talented actress, Sienna. She can do this thing of having a public smile, but with private worry in the eyes.

She was really great in terms of dealing with the script and talking to me about, “I don’t think she’d quite say it like that,” and we would sit there together on set and tweak stuff. But never did we talk about the meta thing of Sienna Miller in the public glare. She was treating it as a proper part.

“Notting Hill” plays that much more for laughs with Rhys Ifans in his pants. But actually, I felt that [showed] the press physically bearing down on you.

I really wanted to use it as a way to pressurize Sienna, like that door might physically collapse on her. It was a quicker way into just seeing that the weight of the press attention was right there, so it’s a little nod to “Notting Hill,” just in the sheer kind of [gasp] of opening the door. But it’s not used for laughs.

Who was the guy that chased that woman down the corridor? It was awful.

I think it’s more that sort of stuff happens in hotels, and there’s CCTV in hotels. You’re always looking in thrillers to find ways you could kind of revisit. So it wasn’t trying to plug into any one particular incident.

I wanted to make it a bit different because — full disclosure — I didn’t want Olivia Wilde to think we were just aping her experience. But also, I thought by distancing ourselves from it, we could make it even worse for Carla by putting it right in her biggest night, her biggest moment. This really is the culmination. She’s on a career comeback at that point. She’s been in some less good movies than “The Milliner,” if you can imagine, and so this was a really special night for her. So using that for dramatic effect felt good.

But the thing that I was really inspired by the Olivia Wilde thing was just how professional and upset she was at the same time. Like we’re talking about Sienna’s ability to [show] the pain it’s caused, and then you see how she’s painting on a smile quickly to get through it. And I really spotted that in Olivia Wilde’s way of dealing with that in that moment, which I thought that just reflected very well on Olivia Wilde. So I was happy to be inspired by that because it’s probably an awful moment in someone’s life, and you’ve got to be choose the right way to kind of be inspired.

There were lots of fun names. The script editor came up with a brilliant list of films that are sort of mid-level Euro, kind of awards worthy, like “The Red Violinist of Marseille” and things like that, or you know, “The Harpist of Madrid.” But in the end, it was just “The Milliner.” We had this story that she was undercover in occupied Europe with a hat.

The thing we look for in the divorce [case] is to analyze the relationships of all the lawyers. And with the libel case, it’s all about reputation. How our reputations are affecting us at work and what our work reputation means to us in life is a really cool thing to have exposed by a libel trial. So it’s about playing those two things off against each other, and bringing in a few new characters and a few new bits of casting to make it believable that what we see as predominantly family lawyers can also have other people in their departments who have libel experience.

Nick Mohammed really comes through in Season 2, which was again part of our pledge to him. And there’s a new hiring at Cathcarts, who starts to — not cause problems, but definitely shakes up the dynamic.

Tonally, it’s adjacent but it’s not exactly the same. And we want to have different aspects. It’s a different album.

I’m actually looking the other way. I’m looking for the thing that affects people and then looking for the legal equivalent. It’s the relationship stories that we’ve seen in movies or in our own lives that we really relate to on an emotional level, and then quickly scouring law to say, “Well, where is that felt?” So it’s less with Walliams or Russell Brand. Like, I’m interested in how the justice system will treat those people, but I’m not using that for inspiration for drama because I don’t know how it feels for anyone involved because I don’t know it. So we’re trying to start with the feeling.

Google rolls out new search homepage with AI chat history and no search button

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google search is making it even easier to access AI tools on the search page.

Google’s flagship search experience is evolving into an AI experience with stunning speed. Even time-tested elements of search are getting AI makeovers, and the latest to get this treatment is the search homepage on Google.com. A redesign that’s been in testing for the last few weeks is now official, bringing easier access to AI chats and dropping the dedicated search button entirely.

Google search VP Robby Stein confirmed on X that Google is deploying the new desktop search page after a successful test. The page will now have a few more buttons under the search bar, all connected to AI features. There’s an image generation button, one for analyzing files, and one for brainstorming with Gemini. The “I’m Feeling Lucky” button remains, but the Google Search button that used to sit next to it is gone.

The search button has obviously been a part of Google’s search page forever. However, it wasn’t in a very convenient location in the modern interface. When you begin typing a query, the autocomplete suggestions cover that button. You have to click out of the text field to access the button, which is an extra step compared to simply mashing the Enter key to run your search.

The other big change brings your recent AI conversations to the forefront. If you’re logged into Google, the search homepage will have an expandable sidebar on the left with your recent AI Mode search threads. This sidebar will also be accessible on the results page. According to Stein, the sidebar made a “big difference” in how people made progress on their web searches, calling it a “simpler way to pick up where you left off.”

It’s easy to feel frustrated by changes like this if you don’t like Google’s new AI approach to search. But at the same time, the loss of functionality is minimal. Google probably has usage data that shows that particular search button was rarely used. Most people probably just hit Enter or click an autocomplete suggestion to go to the results.

This change says more about Google’s priorities and the future of search. AI features get prominent buttons, and your AI conversations now have a whole sidebar UI. Meanwhile, traditional search is increasingly de-emphasized. There may come a day when AI Mode takes over completely, forcing you to manually select the old-fashioned version of search. It does feel like Google is inching in that direction.

Denmark accuses Russia of plotting to assassinate US-Ukrainian rapper

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Denmark has accused Russian intelligence services of plotting to assassinate a Ukrainian-American near Copenhagen, saying they had "planned" and "initiated" the attempt.

Denmark's security and intelligence agency said it had stopped individuals who were intending to kill the person, named by Danish media as rapper Kyivstoner, at a concert in September. Two people were charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service, and pleaded not guilty.

Denmark's foreign minister said Russia's defence attachÃ© will now be expelled.

Russia's Embassy in Denmark called the allegations "unsubstantiated" and said Copenhagen failed to provide evidence of Moscow's involvement.

In a statement posted on social media, the embassy claimed a "Danish model" had emerged, which it described as a "Russian trace" allegedly being discovered and "exploited for a propaganda spectacle". It accused Denmark of "provocations" intending to "frighten the Danish population in order to justify... a prolonged confrontation with Russia".

Russian state media cites the foreign ministry in Moscow as saying it will respond to the expulsion of its official "accordingly".

Danish media named the target of the assassination attempt as the rapper known as Kyivstoner, whose real name is Albert Vasiliev.

Denmark's security and intelligence agency (PET) said the individuals who allegedly tried to assassinate him were associated with a banned Danish gang named Loyal to Familia. The group is "known for extensive, serious and violent criminal activity", according to the agency.

PET, which worked with police on the case, said three individuals were stopped while travelling by car near the music venue where Kyivstoner was set to perform on 25 September.

Firearms and knives were found in the vehicle, PET said.

Since then, two people linked to the case have been charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service.

Both individuals pleaded not guilty to the charges, local media reported.

In July, Russia's FSB security service accused Vasiliev and the Ukrainian authorities of organising a "terrorist attack" on military facilities and personnel in Russia. The rapper denied this allegation on social media.

In a statement on Friday, Danish Foreign Minister Lars LÃ¸kke Rasmussen said the alleged assassination attempt was Russian "intimidation" and an attempt to make Denmark "waver" in its support for Ukraine.

Russia's defence attachÃ© must leave the country within seven days, Rasmussen said.

He also said Denmark wants the EU to restrict the ability of Russians to travel throughout Europe, saying even though Denmark is already very reluctant to issue visas, Russians can still get a Schenegen visa in another country and travel to Denmark. Denmark wants these visas to apply only in the country that has issued them, he added.

Nvidia stock plunges 14%, wraps up longest weekly losing streak since 2008 financial crisis

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The last time Wall Street was this down on Nvidia the chipmaker's flagship product was the GeForce graphics processor for high-end PC video games, and markets were reeling from the global financial crisis.

Nvidia shares tumbled 14% on Friday, sliding for a seventh straight day as investors grew increasingly concerned about life after co-founder Jensen Huang. The stock closed lower for a seventh consecutive week, the longest such stretch since 2008.

Shares of Nvidia are down 46% over the period, pushing the company's market cap down by about $2.6 trillion. The stock is now trading at roughly 8.4 times forward earnings, while Advanced Micro Devices is valued at 29 times profit for the next 12 months.

On Friday, Reuters reported that the Securities and Exchange Commission is exploring potential accounting issues at Nvidia, pushing the stock down further.

Once the most valuable company in the world, Nvidia now has a market cap of $3 trillion, putting it fourth behind Apple, Microsoft and Alphabet.

Nvidia is reeling from the removal of Huang, who resigned on Nov. 12, after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly a spy.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said earlier this week that Huang had resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

But the chipmaker's losing streak started before Nvidia was thrown into crisis as investors worried over the sustainability of the AI buildout, particularly after the midterm elections, and emerging competition in AI chips from Google and AMD.

Even a better-than-expected earnings report, which included an increase to fiscal 2028 guidance, didn't stem the selling. Interim CEO Colette Kress, the company's longtime CFO, projected revenue growth next fiscal year of 75%, and said that customers aren't going anywhere.

On Tuesday's earnings call with analysts, Kress signed off by saying, "we've got this." In a company meeting the next day, executives told staff that it was moving its yearly review process, which can come with more stock, from March to December.

For investors, one major question is whether Nvidia can continue to innovate and sustain its commanding lead in the AI chip market without its visionary leader. Almost the entire company now is built around the AI trade, a phenomenon that began taking hold just four years ago with the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT.

Before that, Nvidia's primary business was selling graphics cards used for video games. In mid-2008, the last time the stock was this deep into a weekly losing streak, Nvidia was generating less than $1 billion per quarter. Now it pulls in more than $1 billion per day. At the time, the entire market was in freefall due to the worst financial crisis since the Great Depression.

AWS Routes HyperPod Inference Across Regions From One Endpoint

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AWS introduced Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Global Inference Gateway on November 20, 2026, giving customers one managed endpoint for inference requests across registered HyperPod clusters in a single AWS Region or multiple Regions. The service selects a cluster using capacity, queue, proximity and error signals, with automatic failover when a cluster becomes unavailable.

The launch adds a second routing tier above the existing SageMaker HyperPod Inference Gateway. That cluster-level gateway continues to select individual model pods using signals including key-value (KV) cache utilization and queue depth; the new global tier selects the cluster first, while leaving pod selection unchanged.

AWS is addressing a capacity-utilization problem: requests can queue on a saturated cluster while already-provisioned GPUs sit idle elsewhere. In its example of four models served across five clusters in two Regions, per-model serving requires clients to track 20 endpoints. Cluster-level gateways cut that to five, while the global gateway provides one. The managed tier removes the need to build and maintain load balancers, DNS and cross-Region networking for this routing, and lets peak traffic overflow to clusters with available capacity.

An operator publishes each participating cluster by enabling spec.globalRouting in its existing InferenceGatewayConfig, then calls CreateGlobalInferenceGateway with the clusters to group. Publishing creates a Network Load Balancer, a virtual private cloud (VPC) endpoint service and certificate resources in the customer’s account. An unpublished cluster cannot be added successfully.

Clients send OpenAI-compatible requests signed with AWS Signature Version 4 (SigV4) to the regional endpoint. The gateway authenticates the caller and selects a healthy cluster, AWS PrivateLink carries the request within or across Regions, and the cluster’s Inference Gateway chooses the model pod. SageMaker operates the routing fleet, private endpoints, certificate validation, health checks, authentication and rate limiting; a service-managed publisher on each cluster supplies load metrics.

For each request, the gateway scores healthy clusters using four configurable signals:

Customers overriding the defaults must set all four weights, using values from 0.0 to 1.0 that total 1.0, and can disable a signal with zero. UpdateGlobalInferenceGateway changes the weighting for new requests without reprovisioning, while in-flight requests finish under the old settings. AWS says workloads that prioritize resilience over proximity can raise ErrorRate and lower RegionProximity.

The gateway health-checks every cluster and shifts traffic to the next-best cluster after a failure without requiring client changes, bringing a recovered cluster back gradually. A failed request is retried once on another cluster unless streaming has started; if no healthy cluster remains, the gateway returns HTTP 503. Adding a healthy cluster does not change the endpoint or disrupt other clusters, while removing one stops new requests but lets in-flight work finish.

IAM separately controls standard and streaming invocation, and customer JSON Web Tokens, when used, travel inside the SigV4 signature. Requests reach clusters only over AWS PrivateLink, and AWS states that the gateway does not log or store prompts or completions. Deleting the global gateway stops its endpoint but leaves the per-cluster endpoints serving.

Bagelcode VP of AI Platform Jinhyung Park said in AWS’s announcement that beta tests routed requests across two Regions through the gateway and continued serving them when one cluster could no longer serve the model. That gave the company’s small platform team cross-Region failover without building or maintaining its own routing layer.

The service is available in 13 Regions: US East (N. Virginia and Ohio), US West (N. California and Oregon), South America (São Paulo), Europe (Frankfurt, Ireland, London and Stockholm), and Asia Pacific (Tokyo, Mumbai, Singapore and Sydney).

AWS advises single-cluster users to remain on the cluster Inference Gateway endpoint and adopt the global service when adding a second cluster. Existing per-cluster endpoints continue serving during client migration, and a gateway containing only clusters in one Region can keep a workload there. A published cluster left outside any gateway receives no traffic even though its Network Load Balancer continues to run and incur charges.

By putting proximity, capacity and failure signals in one policy, the service makes the use of otherwise idle GPU capacity a configuration decision rather than a separate networking project.

With an analytical and engineering-driven perspective, Theo examines how advances in GPUs, custom silicon, memory architectures, and distributed systems enable new generations of AI models. Potential Cognition AI Round Would Raise $2B-Plus at $75B Valuation

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bloomberg News reported on November 20, 2026, citing people familiar with the matter, that Cognition AI was discussing a new funding round at a $75 billion valuation and considering raising $2 billion or more, in an account carried by Reuters. The talks were ongoing and the details could change.

The report described a potential financing rather than a completed investment. Cognition did not immediately respond to Reuters’ request for comment.

Cognition announced its Series E on September 8, 2026, raising more than $2 billion at a $48 billion valuation. New investors Andreessen Horowitz and Accel led the round, joined by existing backers Founders Fund, General Catalyst and Avenir. The proposed figure is $27 billion higher than that completed valuation, calculated as the difference between the two stated valuations.

The earlier May 27, 2026 financing raised more than $1 billion at a $26 billion valuation and was led by Lux Capital, General Catalyst and 8VC. The completed May and September rounds provide reference points for the new report, while the latest terms remain under negotiation.

Reuters describes Cognition’s autonomous software-engineering agents as able to handle code planning, writing, testing and deployment with limited human intervention. Devin is Cognition’s product for this work.

Cognition’s own disclosures report run-rate revenue growth from $492 million in May to almost $900 million by September, alongside enterprise usage growth of more than tenfold between the start of 2026 and the May announcement. The revenue increase is roughly $400 million, calculated from the two reported run-rate figures.

Cognition says Devin is used by engineering teams at NVIDIA, GE Aerospace, Citi, Mercedes-Benz and Modal, spanning chip design, aviation, financial services, automotive and AI infrastructure. Devin Auto-Triage can handle an initial incident investigation, Devin Security Swarm identifies and prioritizes vulnerabilities, and Devin Automations can begin tasks in response to activity in Slack, GitHub, Linear and other systems without requiring a separate chat for each task.

The September announcement also said Cognition had opened offices in Washington, D.C., Tokyo, Singapore, London, São Paulo and Madrid during the preceding year, alongside its existing San Francisco, New York and Austin hubs. The office expansion accompanied the company’s reported revenue growth and broader customer adoption.

Cognition also describes itself as an independent agent lab that can select and combine models for particular tasks, including its own, so customers are not confined to a single provider. That approach makes model choice part of Devin’s product design as the company expands into incident response, security and event-triggered automation.

Until a transaction is announced, the open question is whether the growth and broader Devin deployment documented in September can support the proposed terms, or whether the negotiations produce a different valuation or funding amount.

CrowdStrike Reportedly in Talks to Acquire AI Security Partner Zscaler

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bloomberg reported on November 20, 2026, that CrowdStrike was in ongoing talks to acquire Zscaler, citing people familiar with the matter, in a report carried by CNBC. Zscaler shares rose 18% in extended trading following the report, while CrowdStrike shares fell more than 8%.

No acquisition agreement has been announced. CrowdStrike declined to comment on the report, and Zscaler did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

CNBC put CrowdStrike’s market capitalization at $235 billion after its stock roughly doubled during 2026, while Zscaler’s was about $32 billion after a 13% decline during the year.

A completed transaction would rank among cybersecurity’s largest, CNBC said. Its comparisons were Google’s $32 billion acquisition of Wiz in 2025, Cisco’s roughly $28 billion purchase of Splunk in 2024 and Palo Alto Networks’ $25 billion acquisition of CyberArk in 2026.

Zscaler’s September 2, 2026 partnership announcement details an existing technical relationship between the companies. The expanded partnership is intended to automate security workflows and support real-time, adaptive Zero Trust enforcement by connecting CrowdStrike’s detection and response with Zscaler’s access controls.

Zscaler said a planned integration using the OpenID Shared Signals Framework, an industry-standard method for sharing risk context, would let Falcon continuously evaluate user risk and pass that context to Zscaler’s Adaptive Access Engine. When suspicious endpoint behavior is detected, Zscaler can then automatically adjust access to match the current risk posture.

The companies are also developing a native Falcon Foundry application built on Zscaler OneAPI. Planned functions include triggering Zscaler enforcement from Charlotte Agentic SOAR, CrowdStrike’s security automation interface, and supporting end-to-end automated remediation without middleware, connectors or separate infrastructure.

Zscaler said network-event and AI-event logs are designed to stream into Falcon Next-Gen SIEM, CrowdStrike’s security-event management platform, where they can be correlated with Falcon endpoint, identity and cloud data. The combined telemetry is designed to help analysts reconstruct multi-stage attacks across domains. The AI telemetry includes AI-application interactions, risky chatbot prompts and policy violations, placing AI-specific risks alongside network and user activity in a unified investigation interface.

Under the partnership plan, CrowdStrike would also expand Project QuiltWorks, an industry coalition focused on frontier AI risk, to include Zscaler. Zscaler cautioned that unreleased services and features covered by the announcement remained in development and subject to change.

On November 3, 2026, CrowdStrike announced a definitive agreement to acquire Fiddler AI to add pre-deployment evaluation, continuous agent observability and explainability to Falcon. A separate agreement to acquire Dreadnode would add automated pre-deployment security testing of AI models, applications and agents. Both proposed acquisitions were expected to close in CrowdStrike’s fourth quarter of fiscal 2027, subject to customary closing conditions.

The reported talks sit on top of an existing integration roadmap rather than a relationship built from scratch. The open question is whether that roadmap remains a partner integration or becomes part of a single company; for now, the documented product work and the reported transaction are at different stages, with several of the partnership’s features still in development.

Miles Okada is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, covering artificial intelligence and cybersecurity with a focus on emerging threats, defensive architectures, and the evolving dynamics between attackers and automated systems. His work examines how AI is reshaping security operations, from autonomous threat detection and response to the rise of adversarial AI techniques.

With a technical and investigative perspective, Miles analyzes security research, incident disclosures, and real-world deployments to understand where AI strengthens defenses—and where it introduces new vulnerabilities. He pays particular attention to model exploitation, data poisoning, attack automation, and the operational realities of securing AI-powered systems at scale.

GTA 6 Smashes Multiple Sales Records And Earns Xbox Series S Praise In First 24 Hours

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Update: "Grand Theft Auto VI day one retail sales reached $3.79 billion"

Update [Fri 20th Nov 2026, 10:02pm]: GTA 6 publisher Take-Two is now reporting that day one sales reached $3.79 billion USD, while also selling 32.5 million units pre-release. The $3.79 billion figure is based on "company estimates".

"Rockstar Games, a label of Take-Two Interactive Software, Inc. (NASDAQ: TTWO), is proud to announce that Grand Theft Auto VI day one retail sales reached $3.79 billion, setting a new record for entertainment across any medium, and becoming the biggest entertainment launch in history."

"Achieving a near perfect score of 99 on Metacritic and resounding critical acclaim, Grand Theft Auto VI sold 32.5 million units pre-release, a new record across all platforms."

Original Story: Rockstar's Grand Theft Auto 6 has reportedly smashed all sorts of records within 24 hours of launch, and it's off to a great start on Xbox Series consoles.

As shared by GTA 6 Countdown, GTA 6 has launched to a near-perfect 99 score on Metacritic (151 reviews), apparently sold 24.5m copies in under 24 hours, and seen 2 million Xbox players take a trip to New Zealand to play the game early.

Sensor Tower's sales tracker also estimates $2.36bn (£1.8bn) in revenue from GTA 6 sales, with the console split mostly in favour of PS5, at a 75% to 25% share over Xbox. Still, the data suggests Xbox still took a whopping $588m from 6.15m units sold.

GTA 6 dominated Twitch viewership too, with 50m hours watched and 3.3m viewers during its busiest hour, beating the livestreaming site's previous record (held by Hogwarts Legacy at 1.2m) by more than double.

Beyond all the big numbers we're chucking your way, we've also spotted a lot of praise for the Xbox Series S version of the game. Whilst Digital Foundry was hit-and-miss on the report, the general response online is majorly positive.

It's good news, then, to finally put to bed the lingering question of whether GTA 6 would run well enough on Xbox Series S consoles. Judging by IGN's comparison, it's not all that different of an experience to Sony's flagship PS5 Pro.

@HonestHick LGs and OLEDs are great, but every TV technology has pros and cons, there isn't a perfect one. E.g. OLED isn't as bright so may not be the best choice in brighter rooms. And for lower frame rates, like 30fps games, it often looks choppier on OLED because the screen is too responsive and you notice it more. OLED amplifies that lower frame rate, whereas LCDs slightly blurring between frames with "sample-and-hold" smooths the transitions. The thing that is bad at high frame rates, becomes a benefit at low frame rates.

Personally I found playing the Switch much less pleasant on my LG OLED, before it broke, and actually ended up playing most Switch games on another TV because of this. (They also looked better on a smaller screen)

But for faster frame rates the motion clarity is much better (on the high end LG models) as are deeper blacks.

@HonestHick As for refunds I probably have a very unpopular take on this. But I don't think we should just be able to get refunds by default if we simply don't like a video game. If I go to the theatre, cinema or sporting event and I don't like it, if my team lost, I don't typically get a refund. If I buy a movie and don't like it I don't usually get a refund. Why should we for games?

The one exception for me is performance. If a game doesn't perform well, or makes us motion sick, or similar then yes there should be that option, but I don't think, just because you don't like a game, there should be an expectation of a refund.

Steam is slightly different as every PC runs differently and you may need 2 hours to see if a game works at all on your hardware. Consoles on the other hand everybody gets the same performance. But if a game is broken, or performs poorly on your hardware, e.g. CyberPunk on XBO/PS4, then it should be easier.

"In difesa, per esempio, continua a essere seguita la situazione del 26enne statunitense Chris Richards del Crystal Palace - utilizzabile sia da centrale che da braccetto destro -; mentre a centrocampo il club nerazzurro sta valutando André Frank Zambo Anguissa del Napoli. Il centrocampista camerunese, che il 16 novembre ha compiuto 31 anni, ha il contratto fino al 30 giugno 2027 e le contrattazioni fra il Napoli e il suo agente Maxine Nana non hanno fin qui prodotto novità (...). Adesso Anguissa per prolungare è partito da richieste alte, vicine ai 6 milioni".

"L'Inter sta ovviamente studiando il fascicolo del centrocampista che ha quelle caratteristiche che mancano da anni nel reparto mediano nerazzurro; quell'elemento "alla Koné" cercato - invano - per due estati. In estate sembra scontato l'addio e l'Inter ci pensa, anche perché Chivu perderà Mkhitaryan, Calhanoglu per ora rimane in scadenza 2027 e Stankovic dopo un'annata ai margini potrebbe partire per andare per andare a giocare di più".

The Customer Paid. Then Silence: Five Updates a Small Business Forgets to Send

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The customer paid on Monday. By Friday, silence. The update lives in your head, not in a system — you meant to send it, you know exactly what it would have said, and the week filled up before you sent it. The customer is not angry yet. They are doing something worse: they are wondering. Wondering whether the money arrived, whether the work started, whether the date still holds, whether anyone is there at all. A paid customer who hears nothing starts to feel like a forgotten one, and a forgotten customer is one message away from becoming someone else's customer.

This article was drafted with AI assistance. It is written in plain English for people who run businesses, not for people who build software, and it makes no claim about any particular product. It is about the question this morning's post asked — what update is still waiting? — and it works through five updates a small business forgets to send, using the tools people already use, so you can check my working and tell me where I got it wrong. This is the twenty-seventh morning we have written about this gap, and the gap is still the same: the work gets done, and the telling does not.

A small business does not have a follow-up problem. It has five follow-up problems, in five places, and each one looks small on its own. The confirmation is only a sentence. The progress note is only a line. The delay warning is only a message. But a customer who gets none of the five hears nothing at all, and nothing at all sounds exactly like nobody cares — even when the work is going perfectly.

Here are the five updates, numbered, each one with the tools people already use. Each one starts with something ordinary. Each one ends with the same question: who was supposed to hear this?

The money arrived on Monday. The customer knows they paid; what they do not know is whether you know. A payment confirmation is the shortest message in business — we got it, thank you, here is what happens next — and it is the one most often skipped, because the person who should send it already knows the money arrived. The customer does not know that you know. For three days they carry a small doubt: did it go through, did it go to the right place, did anyone notice?

WhatsApp is where this message belongs, because WhatsApp is where the customer already is. A sentence in the same thread as the order, the same day as the payment, closes the loop before the doubt starts. A paper receipt does the same job for the customer standing in front of you — it is proof in their hand, and it costs ten seconds. The confirmation is not paperwork. It is the moment the customer stops wondering and starts trusting, and it takes less time than the wondering does.

The job is in progress. Someone is working on it, the parts are ordered, the first step is done. The customer knows none of this, because nobody told them. From their side, the silence after payment looks identical to the silence before anything happened. They paid on Monday, and by Wednesday they have no evidence that anything changed — so they send the message every owner dreads: "Just checking in — any news?"

That message is not a complaint. It is a customer doing your job for you, because the update never arrived. A short note on the day the work starts — we have started, here is the rough shape of the week, I will message again when the next step lands — turns a week of wondering into a week of waiting, and waiting is comfortable when someone said what is happening. Gmail does this well for the customers who live in email; WhatsApp does it better for the ones who live in chat. The tool matters less than the timing: the update has to arrive before the customer has to ask for it, or it is not an update at all.

This is the update that does the most damage when it is missing, and the one owners put off longest, because nobody enjoys sending bad news. The date moved. The part is late, the diary slipped, the supplier has not called back. The customer still has the old date in their head, and every day that passes without a new one makes the old date feel like a promise that was broken on purpose.

A delay notice sent early is not bad news. It is respect. "The date has moved to Thursday, here is why, here is what I am doing about it" turns a broken promise into a kept one — the customer was told, in time, by the person doing the work. A delay notice sent late, or never, turns the same delay into a discovery: the customer finds out on the day the job was supposed to happen, from the silence, and no explanation after that sounds like one. Send this one first, before the others, and send it the moment you know. The customer can forgive a moved date. They cannot forgive finding out last.

The work is finished. The order is packed, the repair is done, the document is ready to collect. The customer does not know, because the finished job is sitting in the same silence as the unfinished one. This is the strangest of the five, because the hard part is over and the only thing left is a sentence — and the sentence is the thing that gets forgotten, because the person who finished the job has already moved on to the next one.

"It's ready, here is how to collect it, here is what to check" is a message that ends the job properly. Without it, the customer waits for a call that never comes, and the finished work waits on a shelf for a customer who does not know to come. The notebook on the counter holds the jobs that are done; the message in the chat is the one that tells the customer. A finished job nobody is told about is a job that is not finished yet, in the only way that matters to the person who paid for it.

A week later, nobody has asked. The job was done, the money was paid, the customer went quiet — and the silence after a finished job reads, from the customer's side, exactly like the silence after a forgotten one. They do not know whether you remember them, whether you would notice if something went wrong, whether they are a customer or a transaction that closed.

The check-in is the cheapest message of the five, and the one with the longest memory. "How did it go? Anything I should fix?" takes one sentence, and it is the sentence that turns a one-time buyer into a returning one — not because of what it says, but because it proves someone was still thinking about them after the money was in. It is also the sentence that finds the small problem before it becomes the lost customer: the thing that was slightly wrong, the question that never got asked, the customer who was quietly unhappy and would never have said so unprompted. A business that checks in hears the truth while it is still small.

This is the kind of work an AI workforce is for — not the clever part, the ordinary part. The payment lands and the confirmation is drafted the same day. The work starts and the note is written before the customer has to ask. The date moves and the warning goes out the moment it is known, not the day the customer discovers it. The job finishes and the "it's ready" is ready to send. A week later, the check-in is drafted and waiting. Five silences become five messages. And nothing is sent before you have seen it. You read the draft, you fix a line if it needs fixing, you say yes — and the customer hears from you the same day, not the next week.

The relief is not that a machine wrote faster. It is that the telling stopped depending on a free minute at the end of a long day. You still decide what counts as ready, what counts as late, and what counts as worth saying. You still approve every word that leaves. The difference is that the drafting is done before you sit down, so the minutes you have are spent deciding — not remembering, at 11pm, the five messages that never went out.

The delay notice goes first, because it is the only one where silence actively does harm. A customer who is not told the date moved will find out on the day the job was supposed to happen, and that discovery costs more than any delay ever could. Tell them the moment you know, and the delay becomes a plan instead of a surprise.

The payment confirmation goes next, because it is the easiest to send and the quickest to earn trust — one sentence, the same day as the money, and the customer stops wondering. "We've started" follows, because it stops the "just checking in" message before it is sent. "It's ready" comes after, because a finished job that nobody is told about is not finished yet. The check-in goes last, not because it matters least, but because it only works once the other four have been sent: a business that never confirmed the payment and never warned about the delay has not earned the right to ask how it went.

That order is my answer. It is also a guess, and it is the part I most want corrected.

After a customer pays, five updates go missing: the payment confirmation, the "we've started" note, the delay warning, the "it's ready" message, and the check-in a week later. Each one is a sentence, each one lives in a tool people already use — WhatsApp, Gmail, a phone call, a paper receipt, a notebook — and the gap is between the job and the telling, and the owner is the glue. Let a workforce draft the five messages as the work happens, and keep the sending in your hands: you pick the order, you approve every word, and the customer stops wondering.

So — what update is still waiting? Which of these five is yours, and which would you send first? Tell me in the comments. I would rather be corrected than agreed with.

Ready-made AI agents for small businesses, built in public.

A Default-Deny NAT Firewall on OpenBSD pf, Managed with Terraform

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at blog.bsdserver.nl

pf is the best firewall I have worked with: one readable file, atomic reloads, NAT and queueing in the same language as the filter rules. What it lacks is an API. Every tool that wants to change a pf firewall ends up templating pf.conf, copying it over SSH and hoping pfctl -f likes it.

I wrote pfapid to fix that: a small REST API for pf that runs on the firewall itself, with a Terraform provider on top. This post builds a complete NAT firewall with it: a default-deny base, outbound NAT, a port forward to a DMZ web server, a blocklist and QoS on the uplink.

pfapid splits the firewall into two owners:

The base ruleset is the part you can't afford to get wrong, so the API can't touch it. Whatever Terraform does, the block and management rules in pf.conf come first.

The rules use interface groups (wan, lan, dmz, mgmt), never device names. When you replace a NIC or move a network to a VLAN, you add the new interface to the group and no rule changes:

pfapid ships as signed release archives for OpenBSD amd64 and arm64. Check the signature before you install it:

gpg must report a good signature from key 10A2F1058821D55C61E45C7EE97C02FEE2F51B23. Then on the firewall, as root:

The three empty files must exist before pf.conf includes them; pfapid fills them later.

pfapid has a built-in CA, so you don't need a PKI to get started. Create the CA and the server certificate, then a client certificate for Terraform:

Copy /root/tf-firewall.crt, /root/tf-firewall.key and the CA certificate (/etc/pfapid/pki/ca.crt) to the machine that runs Terraform, and delete the key from the firewall. A company CA, Vault or SPIFFE IDs work too; see CERTIFICATES.md.

This file is yours. It holds everything that must hold no matter what the API does:

Check the file, then load it with a way back. I locked myself out of my lab firewall while writing this post: my workstation wasn't in admin_nets. A background job that restores the old file after 90 seconds unless I cancel it brought the firewall back on its own:

The test has to be a new connection: existing SSH sessions keep their state and survive a rule change that would lock out the next login.

/etc/pfapid.json (mode 0600, root) lists the interface groups, tables and queue devices the API may use, and which client may do what:

The provider is signed with the same key as the pfapid releases; terraform init checks that for you.

Tables are lists of addresses that rules refer to as . Changing a table never reloads a rule, so it's the cheap way to add a web server or block a network. A table can hold IPv4 and IPv6 entries side by side:

Each pf_anchor is one anchor, api/, and its rules are loaded as a whole: pf is order-sensitive, so a ruleset is one atomic unit, never rule by rule. Split anchors along ownership: whoever owns the DMZ web server owns dmz-web.

The rdr-to rule changes the destination of connections to the wan address on port 80 or 443 to 10.0.20.10. The same pass rule creates the state, so there is no separate redirect-then-filter step to keep in sync. The redirected packet then leaves on dmz, and the web-out rules let it through: forget those and the port forward silently goes nowhere. Those same rules let LAN clients reach the web server directly.

For IPv6 the server has its own address, so there is nothing to translate: pass it in on wan and out on dmz.

quick ends evaluation on a match, so these blocks win over every pass rule in every anchor, whatever order pf evaluates the anchors in. log sends the dropped packets to pflog0.

Feed the table from wherever your threat data lives: a variable, a file, or a data source. Updating it is a table replace on the firewall, not a ruleset reload.

An upload that fills the uplink makes everything else on the line feel slow: VoIP breaks up, SSH lags, and downloads crawl because their ACKs queue behind the upload. HFSC queues fix that by sharing the uplink by rule:

Each anchor's plan includes pf_rules: the rules exactly as pf will load them, as pfctl -nv on the firewall printed them. This is what the plan showed in my lab (the QoS root was on vio1 there):

Lists become one line per port and per prefix, and pf adds its defaults (flags S/SA, round-robin). You see that before anything changes, not after.

Each change was loaded, confirmed by the provider over the same connection, and only then written to pfapid's state. A second terraform plan reports no changes.

Drift. I flushed an anchor by hand with pfctl -a api/dmz-web -F rules. The next plan:

The management interface. A rule that opens RDP on mgmt fails at plan time:

A rule pf won't take. pfapid runs pfctl -n during the plan, so a rule pf would refuse fails terraform plan, with pf's own error message, before anything is loaded.

Everything in this post was planned and applied with provider v0.1.0 and pfapid v0.1.2 on an OpenBSD 7.9 lab VM, and the pf output shown is what pf loaded there. The code, the design notes and the pf behaviour pfapid relies on are on GitHub: pfapid and terraform-provider-pf. Issues and pull requests are welcome.

The AI SDK's MCP drift check doesn't hash readOnlyHint: what that would miss, counted on 14,009 servers

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at modelmarket.dev

Since version 7.0.19 the Vercel AI SDK can fingerprint the tools of an MCP server. You call fingerprintTools when you first trust a server, store the result, and on later connects detectToolDrift tells you which tools were added, removed or changed. The package it ships in, ai, was downloaded 48.3 million times in the week of 12–18 November 2026.

The fingerprint covers three members of a tool definition: the description, the input schema and the title. Annotations are not among them. A tool that said readOnlyHint: true on the day you reviewed it can say readOnlyHint: false and destructiveHint: true later, and the drift check returns three empty lists.

We run HISTOR, a public record of what MCP servers advertise and when it changes, so we counted how often that happens. Between 23 October and 16 November 2026, 524 tools on 189 servers switched readOnlyHint from true to false. For 176 of those tools the fingerprint of the tool would not have moved. For 91 of them, on 32 servers, no tool on the server changed its fingerprint in that read, so the check would have had nothing to report.

The gap itself is not our find. NerdLevelTech showed it in September on a purpose-built server, for the output schema as well, and published a fingerprint over the raw tools/list that closes it. What a test server cannot say is how often this happens on servers people actually run. That is the part counted here.

This is read from the published source of ai 7.0.177 (src/generate-text/tool-fingerprint.ts) and @ai-sdk/mcp 2.0.101 (src/tool/mcp-client.ts). In our words, not the library's code:

Two things follow. Everything outside those three members is invisible: outputSchema, the hint annotations, _meta, icons, execution, vendor members. And the top-level additionalProperties of the input schema is replaced with false before hashing, so a server that changes it from false to true hashes the same.

The choice is defensible. The description and the schema are what the model reads, so they are where an injected instruction goes, and the function's own comment calls them the server-controlled, security-relevant fields. Hints are a different kind of risk: the model does not act on them, the host does. And hosts do. The same AI SDK documentation page shows an approval policy that lets a call run without asking when readOnlyHint is true, next to a note that annotations are untrusted hints. Cursor's Bugbot only uses MCP tools that a server marks read-only, according to Cursor staff. Where a host decides like that, the hint is part of what was approved. The release note is explicit about the division of labour: "Baseline storage and the drift response remain the app's responsibility."

A one-tool MCP server over stdio, read through the SDK's own client: once as reviewed, once after only the hints were edited, once after only the description was edited. The three files are server.mjs, pin.mjs and check_ai_sdk.mjs, with a package.json that pins ai 7.0.177, @ai-sdk/mcp 2.0.101 and zod 4.1.8. Node.js 22 or newer:

The first three lines are what the server sent and the fingerprint the SDK computed. With the hints reversed the fingerprint is the same string, and detectToolDrift has nothing to report. The description edit moves it. The last line is the same comparison over a hash of the whole definition, which is the fix further down.

HISTOR reads the tool list of every remote server in the official MCP registry without credentials, on a schedule, and of npm and PyPI server packages once per published version. Since 23 October it keeps every member of a definition, annotations included. The count below uses the snapshot of 16 November: every read between 23 October and 16 November in which something in a tool definition differed from the previous read of the same server.

The middle column is the per-tool view: the drift check may name another tool of that server, but not this one. The right column is the case where it names nothing.

These are not spread evenly. One PyPI package accounts for 37 of the 91 tools, and two hosts for 54 of the 230. They also stay: of the 176 readOnlyHint moves one had gone back by 16 November, and five of the 720 destructiveHint moves.

One case from the data: a remote server whose four tools all went from readOnlyHint: true, idempotentHint: true to false on both in one read, with nothing else changing. That reads like an author correcting annotations that were wrong. It is also exactly the edit that an approval based on the old hints would want to hear about.

Hints are a small part of it. Of 103,657 reads in which a definition changed, 6,338 would move no fingerprint at all: 6.1 % of reads, on 2,014 of 14,009 servers.

Most of these are _meta and outputSchema, and one host produces 2,805 of the 6,338 by itself. We also left out 174,484 reads in which the only thing that moved was a vendor's per-tool version member; counting them would have made the share look far larger and meant nothing. A hash over the whole definition moves on all of that, which is the cost of covering more: more alarms that turn out to be housekeeping.

FastMCP documents a fingerprint too (the "Tool fingerprinting" page, new in 3.0.0). Its base payload is the tool key and the input schema; the page says FastMCP does not define a single contract hash and leaves description, output schema, annotations and _meta for the author to add. It is meant for a server author checking their own tools in CI.

Used as it stands on the consuming side, it would not move in 10,742 reads where only tool descriptions changed, on 4,191 servers. A description edit is the plain form of tool poisoning, so anyone borrowing the recipe for a gateway should add the description first.

detectToolDrift is a plain comparison of two maps, so it works with any fingerprint; the September write-up takes the same route. The client's listTools() returns the definitions as the server sent them (its schema keeps members it does not know); hash those instead:

The last line of the run above is this pin on the hints edit. If the _meta churn of a server you depend on makes it too loud, drop that member before hashing and keep the annotations.

For servers configured in a project file (.mcp.json and the like), WARDEN keeps the same kind of pin in a lock file you commit. Checked on 19 November with the 0.31.0 package from npm and the one-tool server above:

That second command exited 0 while the server was unchanged and 1 after its readOnlyHint went from true to false.

Three files of the snapshot (161 MB), Python 3.9 or newer, no packages:

It prints the JSON the figures are drawn from (results). The file hashes are in the snapshot's signed manifest.json, and the README there says how a change is counted. A second script lists the 321 hint moves of the right-hand column one per line (CSV); it was written separately and arrives at the same 91 and 230.

We run HISTOR and build WARDEN. AI assistance was used for the count, the figures and this article. The data is the HISTOR snapshot of 16 November 2026 (CC BY 4.0); the commands above were run as printed on 19 November 2026.

Blocking GPTBot costs you 40% of your AI citations. The data is in.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The most common GEO advice is "check your robots.txt." Most site owners assume they are fine because they never wrote a Disallow rule for GPTBot. The data says 25.8% of them are wrong.

I ran a pre-registered calibration study in October 2026. The goal was to test whether the GEO score predicts AI citations. It did not. But the study also collected a variable I did not expect to be this important: whether the site blocks at least one AI bot in robots.txt.

341 pages, sampled from the Tranco list, audited with GEO Optimizer 4.19.3. For each page, the tool reads robots.txt and checks whether any of the 27 tracked AI crawlers (GPTBot, ClaudeBot, PerplexityBot, OAI-SearchBot, and 23 more) are explicitly disallowed. The study also queried Perplexity Sonar with two templates per page and recorded whether the domain appeared in the source list.

The result was clear and not part of the pre-registered hypothesis:

That is an 18.6 percentage point gap. Sites that block AI bots get cited roughly 40% less often than sites that do not.

Most of these blocks are not deliberate decisions to keep content out of AI search. The audit data shows three common sources:

Legacy WordPress security plugins. Wordfence and Sucuri ship default rules that block unrecognized user agents. If you installed one of these in 2019 and forgot about it, GPTBot may be getting a 403 right now.

Inherited robots.txt files. Many sites copy a robots.txt from a template or a previous project. If that template was written before AI crawlers existed, it may not have Allow rules for GPTBot or OAI-SearchBot. The crawler falls through to a wildcard Disallow.

CDN bot protection. Cloudflare Bot Fight Mode, when enabled, blocks non-browser user agents at the edge. It does not touch robots.txt, so the block is invisible to any tool that only reads robots.txt. I found that 34.3% of Cloudflare-fronted sites in a 636-site sample block at least one AI crawler at the HTTP level, compared to 12.0% of non-Cloudflare sites.

In a fresh CDN-level scan of 513 reachable sites, the per-bot blocking rates are:

GPTBot is blocked more than any other AI crawler. 12 sites block only GPTBot and nothing else. That selectivity means someone read an article about OpenAI training data and added a Disallow, without realizing that GPTBot also powers ChatGPT's live web search. You can block training and allow citations by separating GPTBot (training) from OAI-SearchBot (search). Most people block both or neither.

14 sites block both GPTBot and PerplexityBot but allow Claude. That is a pattern: site owners who heard about the two most discussed crawlers and blocked them by name, without checking the full list.

If you have never checked whether AI crawlers can reach your site, this is the first thing to fix. Not llms.txt. Not schema. Not content structure. If the crawler gets a 403, every other optimization is invisible.

The fix is a robots.txt audit. Check which of the 27 AI crawlers are explicitly disallowed. If you use Cloudflare, check whether Bot Fight Mode or a WAF rule is intercepting non-browser user agents. If you use a security plugin, check its bot protection settings.

The calibration study is open. The raw data is in the geo-optimizer-skill repo. 341 rows, no domain names, shuffled order. You can reproduce the analysis.

If you want to check your site, run a free audit on geoready.dev. The audit tests all 27 AI crawlers at the robots.txt level and sends live HTTP requests as GPTBot, OAI-SearchBot, PerplexityBot, and Claude-SearchBot to check for CDN-level blocking.

The CLI is on GitHub, MIT licensed, 1,137 stars. pip install geo-optimizer && geo audit https://yoursite.com.

Circle and Trump-linked crypto firm hit with court freeze order after Binance’s $6.3 billion hack

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An Abu Dhabi court orders Circle and 17 firms to maintain freezes on crypto linked to Binance's theft ahead of a Dec. 1 hearing.

An Abu Dhabi court has ordered Circle and 17 other companies to keep tokens linked to Binance’s Oct. 29 theft frozen while the exchange tries to recover them.

Binance disclosed the order on Nov. 18 and said some issuers, exchanges and other partners ask for a court order before they will release or transfer assets they have frozen.

The exchange said the order from the Abu Dhabi Global Market Courts helps open that route, describing ongoing efforts to trace the stolen funds and giving no details of any transfer under the order.

The named firms include Circle, which issues USDC, and World Liberty Financial, the company behind USD1.

Reports noted that the firms must maintain the freezes pending further proceedings and cannot lend, swap, or mix the affected tokens with other assets.

The court did not accuse the firms of wrongdoing, and Dec. 1 is scheduled to consider whether to keep the injunction in place and on what terms.

The injunction covers specific assets Binance claimed are tied to the theft, and said it is still tracing the stolen crypto and taking legal steps to reclaim it. A transfer for firms seeking a court order depends on whether they have authority to release the specific balances they have frozen.

Tether falls outside the order covering the 18 firms, and the company said on Nov. 2 it had frozen more than $1.5 billion tied to the theft.

A spokesperson said Tether would work directly with Binance to reissue the tokens, in consultation with the relevant authorities.

The Abu Dhabi order follows earlier US court action. On Nov. 12, the US Justice Department said it had obtained court authority to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion in stolen crypto. That was part of more than $2.7 billion that US authorities and private firms had helped freeze.

In its Nov. 3 incident report, Binance estimated that about $6.3 billion in digital assets were affected and about $2.85 billion was frozen or intercepted, subject to reconciliation and market prices.

The separate disclosures do not establish how much the figures overlap or how much Binance can ultimately recover. Binance says it absorbed the financial impact on customers while continuing to pursue the stolen assets.

Isaac Asimov’s Book Series Was Unfilmable — Now It's Apple TV's Greatest Space Opera

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sci-fi fans would collectively agree that some stories of the genre are better off staying on the page. For long, it was also believed that Isaac Asimov's books could not be translated to the screen, and for good reason. Especially when it comes to the Foundation book series, it was hard not to believe it was largely "unfilmable."

Some of the greatest sci-fi books are written in a way that it is easy to directly adapt them for the screen. Isaac Asimov's Foundation books, in contrast, defy almost every rule of visual drama. It unfolds less as a fictional drama and more as a deep dive into everything from sociological mechanics to human psychology. While at it, it also spans across centuries, shifting its focus from one character to another.

The books' massive time jumps, anti-cinematic action, and prioritization of ideas over character beats made it impossible to adapt for the screen. This explains why many planned big-budget adaptations of the books also struggled to see the light of day in the past. Fortunately, however, Apple TV's Foundation took a massive risk and achieved the impossible by successfully adapting Isaac Asimov's books.

Impossible feats demand extreme risks. Owing to this, Apple TV's take on the Foundation books significantly changed the source material's approach. The biggest adaptation challenge came from the books' revolving characters. Every few chapters, the books experience time jumps, where old characters die of age and new ones emerge.

On the screen, viewers need rootable visual anchors who appear for longer periods.

Apple TV's Foundation carefully navigated this issue by introducing cloning, cyro-sleep, and digital consciousness. These became narrative devices that allowed the series to logically sustain the same characters and cast members across centuries and seasons. Even an immortal humanoid, named Lady Demerzel, emerges in Foundation from Asimov's wider universe.

Unlike the books, Apple TV's Foundation also visualizes key conflicts and collapses from the original story instead of solely focusing on their political implications. Even "psychohistory" from Isaac Asimov's Foundation gets a visual and more tangible representation in the series.

Armed with the Socratic method, Apple TV's Foundation understands that "The beginning of wisdom is the definition of terms." Instead of treating Asimov's story as a plot to be faithfully transcribed, the show first understands the true essence of its terms. It then turns into a more sprawling space-opera by wisely redefining its ideas.

Many readers have complained that Apple TV's Foundation is too distinct compared to its source material. They argue that it is unfair to even call it a worthy adaptation. It is hard not to agree with them. An adaptation cannot be considered great if it is a far cry from its source material.

Similar to Stanley Kubrick’s The Shining and Denis Villeneuve's Arrival, Foundation fails as a book-to-screen adaptation on many levels.

However, if purely judged as a modern television space opera, Foundation is easily one of the best sci-fi shows of our time. While Asimov's books seemingly argue that historical forces matter far more than individual stories of heroism, Apple TV's Foundation carefully bends the original narrative to focus on almost mythic heroes and solo characters.

It is also arguable that a direct page-to-page TV adaptation of Isaac Asimov's Foundation books would likely have failed. Whether Apple TV's adaptation approach is ideal is still debatable, but it at least gives us an epic sci-fi space opera.

Isaac Asimov's novel series is brought to life in Foundation, a television adaptation of the epic sci-fi saga created for Apple TV+. The show follows a group of exiled humans that find themselves as the last chance to save the Galactic Empire, primarily from itself.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

MLS, la corsa al titolo perde Griezmann: Orlando eliminato negli spareggi da Cincinnati

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Termina nel modo più amaro possibile la prima stagione americana di Antoine Griezmann. Orlando City è stato eliminato nel play-in della MLS, lo spareggio che vale l'accesso ai playoff, perdendo 3-1 contro Cincinnati. L'attaccante francese, arrivato in estate dall'Atletico Madrid, deve così rimandare la conquista del suo primo titolo negli Stati Uniti.

La sfida si era aperta nel migliore dei modi per Orlando, avanti al 26' grazie al gol di Martin Ojeda. Griezmann ha avuto la grande occasione per raddoppiare, ma al 38' la sua conclusione mancina è stata respinta dal portiere Roman Celentano, protagonista con sei parate decisive. Cincinnati ha poi ribaltato il risultato con la doppietta di Evander, prima su rigore e poi con una conclusione a inizio ripresa, chiudendo i conti all'85' con Samuel Gidi. Il francese ha sfiorato anche il 2-2 nei minuti finali, con un colpo di testa terminato vicino alla traversa, ma non è bastato. Nonostante l'eliminazione, il bilancio personale resta straordinario: Griezmann ha chiuso la regular season con 14 gol e 7 assist in 18 partite, venendo anche premiato come miglior nuovo acquisto della MLS.

Nell'altra sfida del play-in sorride il Real Salt Lake, che ha superato i Seattle Sounders ai calci di rigore dopo il pareggio nei tempi regolamentari. La corsa ai playoff MLS entra ora nel vivo: sabato inizieranno gli ottavi e in programma c'è anche la sfida tra Columbus Crew e Inter Miami di Lionel Messi, campione in carica e alla ricerca del secondo titolo consecutivo.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In casa Inter il tema rinnovi è sempre d'attualità. E tra i giocatori che aspettano novità con una certa tre­pi­da­zione c'è Mar­cus Thu­ram, in scadenza nel 2028. Per il francese classe '97 - sempre più indispensabile per Chivu vista anche l'assenza di Lautaro - il primo aspetto da considerare è chiaro: quando sca­drà il con­tratto attuale avrà già 31 anni.

Come potrebbe la società pro­por­gli oggi un'offerta al rialzo? Thu­ram si aspetta di otte­nere un aumento di sti­pen­dio rispetto ai 6 milioni netti (il decreto cre­scita aiuta nella tas­sa­zione il club) che gli ven­gono ver­sati adesso. Solamente, sottolinea in questo senso La Gazzetta dello Sport, la poli­tica della soste­ni­bi­lità impo­sta da Oak­tree ha sta­bi­lito che i nuovi con­tratti deb­bano pre­ve­dere una base fissa non altis­sima abbi­nata a una ricca parte mobile: in altre parole, i cal­cia­tori devono meri­tare i loro emo­lu­menti attra­verso bonus per­so­nali e col­let­tivi che siano deter­mi­nati dal ren­di­mento.

Così Thu­ram, per poter restare all’Inter come desi­dera, dovrà abbas­sare in modo con­si­de­re­vole le pre­tese. Dalla società fil­tra otti­mi­smo, oltre alla deter­mi­na­zione di fis­sare dei paletti finan­ziari. Se non si dovesse rag­giun­gere un’intesa, a giu­gno si potreb­bero fare altri ragio­na­menti. Una proiezione? Thu­ram ha una clau­sola da 85 milioni mai nep­pure avvi­ci­nata da qualche club, tanto che il francese è sem­pre rima­sto feli­ce­mente a Milano. Anche sta­volta può finire così, magari con un con­tratto più lungo e meno pesante da ammor­tiz­zare per l’Inter. Ser­vi­ranno tut­ta­via molti col­lo­qui e tanti incon­tri per far qua­drare i conti nell’inte­resse gene­rale.

China's Xi praises 'turnaround' in relationship with Australia

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The leaders of Australia and China have held talks on sidelines of APEC summit. Australian PM Anthony Albanese is also slated to meet Chinese Premier Li Qiang.

Australian Prime Minister Anthony Albanese and Chinese President Xi Jinping held talks on Friday, following which Xi hailed a "comprehensive turnaround" in the ties between two nations and Albanese said his country was engaging "constructively" with Beijing.

The leaders met on the sidelines of  the Asia-Pacific Economic Forum (APEC) summit in China's Shenzhen.

Xi pointed out to Albanese that they had met four times since the latter became Australia's Prime Minister in 2022.

"I think it is fair to say that together we have brought about a comprehensive ​turnaround in China-Australia relations," the Chinese President said.

Xi also called for enhancing cooperation in agriculture, ​energy, the ‌green economy and artificial intelligence, Chinese state news agency Xinhua reported.

China is Australia's top trading partner.

The relations between the two countries hit rock bottom under Canberra's former centre-right coalition government, which had called for an independent inquiry into the origins of the coronavirus.

An angry China retaliated by imposing import curbs worth $13 billion (€12.2 billion) on Australian timber, coal, barley, lobsters and wine.

Since Albanese's centre-left Labor party came to power, China has lifted most of its blocks on Australian exports.

But friction in ties has surfaced occasionally on issues such as China conducting a missile test in the South Pacific and Australia tightening a  foreign investment screening in the critical minerals sector.

"It is important that Australia continue to engage in a constructive way with China, manage our differences, but not be defined by them," Albanese told the media on Friday.

"What happens in China does matter for Australia," he added.

Albanese is expected to travel to Beijing and meet China's Premier Li Qiang later on Friday.

On Monday, Australia, which is the world's biggest producer of iron ore, called out China's "manipulation" of trade in the commodity over Beijing's formation of a central buying body to purchase ‌ore on behalf of its steelmakers.

A single buyer has increased bargaining power in negotiations with sellers.

Albanese and Li will attend a business roundtable comprising of senior executives from Australian mining giants Rio Tinto and BHP and ​Chinese company executives.

OpenAI is testing Dots personal agents on its $20 per month Plus tier

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dots are also coming to all ChatGPT Business Standard users starting today.

OpenAI is expanding access to Dots, the proactive, always-on AI agents that can toil at tasks for you without any supervision or assistance. After launching the feature for higher-tier plans from ChatGP Pro and up, the company announced that it's testing Dots with a small number of users on the cheaper $20 per month ChatGPT Plus plan.

"Heads up, we're starting to test dots with a small number of ChatGPT Plus users," OpenAI's ChatGPT engineering lead Thibault "Tibo" Sottiaux wrote on X. "Looking forward to the continued feedback as we expand and I'm recently seeing a lot more positives around how good the proactivity has been after many improvements."

Heads up, we're starting to test dots with a small number of ChatGPT Plus users. Looking forward to the continued feedback as we expand and I'm recently seeing a lot more positives around how good the proactivity has been after many improvements.

Dots can interact with over 4,000 apps thanks to their access to OpenAI's plugin ecosystem. The agents can work autonomously on tasks and make progress even when you don't interact with them. They can even connect to your PC, allowing them to control it on your behalf and work alongside you. You can check up on their progress by opening a Dot's "computer" at any time.

Originally powered by GPT-6 Astra, Dots can now run on GPT-6.1 Terra as well. The company just launched several new features for them as well, including iOS widgets and Shortcuts integration, along with iMessage and RCS support, plus iOS notification replies. If you're on a Plus plan, you may gain access starting today, but OpenAI has yet to say whether the upgrade will be permanent.
